.

7.4
Connection to EDS valuations

So far, we have seen that the orders of vanishing obtained using the strategy of
Section

5
agree with the proposed formulas in
Section

2.2
. Just as was the case with singlets, the appearance of these orders of vanishing can at least be partially explained by the results in
StangeEllTrouble
regarding the
p
-adic valuations of elliptic divisibility sequences. However, there is one new complication for the non-singlet cases. The formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
appears to be related to the difference in the
p
-adic valuations of two different elliptic divisibility sequences: one corresponding to the codimension-two behavior and the other corresponding to the codimension-one behavior. Despite this additional difficulty, we can see a clear connection between the proposals and the results in
StangeEllTrouble
.

Fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{su}(n)

As described in
Section

7.1
, we could obtain all the allowed pseudo-charges by starting with a seed model admitting
\bm{n}_{1/n}
matter. In the F-theory constructions we consider, this matter occurs at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}^{(s)}_{n}
to
\text{I}_{n+1}
. Moreover, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(0,1,0,1)
at this locus, indicating that
\mu=1
. Multiples
m\hat{s}
of the generating section support pseudo-charge
\frac{m}{n}
.

If we write this codimension-two locus as
\{\sigma=\Delta_{1/n}=0\}
where
\{\sigma=0\}
is the
\mathfrak{su}(n)
locus, we could imagine an elliptic curve found by setting
\sigma
and
\Delta_{1/n}
to some prime
p
and setting other parameters to some arbitrary integer values. Because the elliptic fibration supports singularity type
\text{I}_{n+1}
at the codimension-two locus, the elliptic curve analogue would have multiplicative reduction modulo
p
. Meanwhile, consider the rational point
P
on the elliptic curve analogue found by making the appropriate substitutions into the generating section expressions. As discussed in
Section

6.2
, the result in
StangeEllTrouble
states that the elliptic divisibility sequence
W^{(2)}_{m}
associated with
P
should have
p
-adic valuations given by an elliptic troublemaker sequence:
29
29

29

We are assuming that no multiples of
P
reduce to the identity point, but this seems to agree with the observed behaviors.

v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})=R_{m}(a,l)\,.

(140)

The integer
l
should equal
v_{p}(\Delta)
, which in this case is
n+1
. Meanwhile,
a
, which describes the component of the Neron fiber hit by
P
, corresponds to
\mu
, which in this case is
1
. We would therefore expect that

v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})=R_{m}(1,n+1)=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{n+1}m^{2}-\frac{\overline{m}_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1-\overline{m}_{n+1}}}\right)}{n+1}}}\right)\,.

(141)

However,
v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})
overcounts
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
. Remember that if
\hat{x}
,
\hat{y}
, and
\hat{z}
are proportional to
A^{2}
,
A^{3}
and
A
for some factor
A
, one can scale the section components and remove this factor of
A
. We always get rid of such factors when calculating the
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
values. However, the recursion formulas for the elliptic divisibility sequences, which are analogous to formulas for the
\hat{z}
components of multiples of the generating section, do not remove such factors. In turn, the formulas from
StangeEllTrouble
count these extra factors as well. This did not cause any issues for the case of singlets, in part because there was no special codimension-one locus in the model. For the
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models, the
\hat{y}
and
\hat{w}
components of the generating section are proportional to
\sigma
. As a result, the formulas for the elliptic divisibility sequences would lead to extra factors of
\sigma
that can be scaled away. To recover
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
, we need to count these extra factors and subtract the result from
v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})
.

We can in fact count these extra factors by considering a second EDS,
W^{(1)}_{m}
, just for the factors of
\sigma
. Imagine we found an elliptic curve by taking our seed model with
\bm{n}_{1/n}
matter and setting
\sigma
(but not
\Delta_{1/n}
) to a prime
p
. The resulting elliptic curve would have multiplicative reduction modulo
p
. If we consider a point found by making the appropriate substitutions into the generating section components, the corresponding EDS valuations should be given by

v_{p}(W^{(1)}_{m})=R_{m}(a,l)\,.

(142)

The integer
l
should equal
v_{p}(\Delta)=n
, while
a
should be given by the value of
\nu
for the generating section, which is
1
. In the end, we have

v_{p}(W^{(1)}_{m})=R_{m}(1,n)=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n-1}{n}m^{2}-\frac{\overline{m}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m}_{n}}}\right)}{n}}}\right)\,.

(143)

The difference of these two sequences of EDS valuations is given by

v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})-v_{p}(W^{(1)}_{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n(n+1)}m^{2}+\frac{\overline{m}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m}_{n}}}\right)}{n}-\frac{\overline{m}_{n+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1-\overline{m}_{n+1}}}\right)}{n+1}}}\right)

(144)

Substituting
m=nq
,
\nu=\overline{m}_{n}
, and
\mu=\overline{m}_{n+1}
, we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle v_{p}(W^{(2)}_{m})-v_{p}(W^{(1)}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n^{2}}{n(n+1)}q^{2}+\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\nu}}\right)}{n}-\frac{\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1-\mu}}\right)}{n+1}}}\right)

(145)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{\SU(n)}}{d_{\SU(n+1)}}q^{2}+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu)-T_{\SU(n+1)}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

This result agrees exactly with observed patterns in
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
and with the proposals in
Section

2.2
.

Antisymmetric representation of
\mathfrak{su}(n)
for odd
n

We saw that all the allowed antisymmetric pseudo-charges for odd
n>4
can be obtained from multiples of the generating section in a model supporting antisymmetric matter with charge
\frac{1}{n}
. Such matter occurs in models with
\nu=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rceil=k+1
. We should therefore be able to obtain the formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
from those in
StangeEllTrouble
describing the EDS valuations. As with fundamental matter, we expect
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
to be given by the difference of EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-two and codimension-one behavior. For the codimension-one
\text{I}_{n}^{(s)}
singularity type, consider the Weierstrass model in
Eq.

124
, which admits an
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model with
n=2k+1
. The locus
\{\sigma=0\}
supports the
\mathfrak{su}(n)
factor, and the locus
\{\sigma=b_{1}=0\}
supports antisymmetric matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
\frac{1}{n}
(up to sign). To construct the EDS associated with the codimension-one behavior, we construct an analogous elliptic curve by setting
\sigma
to a prime integer
p
and the remaining parameters to numbers such that
f
and
g
of the resulting elliptic curve are integers. For instance, we can consider the elliptic curve given by the following Weierstrass equation (with
z
set to 1):

y^{2}=x^{3}-(p-3)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2p^{k}+3p-9}}\right)x-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2(p-3)^{3}-p^{2k}+2(p-3)^{2}p^{k}}}\right)\,.

(146)

This elliptic curve has multiplicative reduction modulo
p
with
v(\Delta)=2k+1
, and it admits the rational point

(x,y)=(3-p,p^{k})\,.

(147)

The rational point reduces to the singular point modulo
p
, and no multiples of this point reduce to the identity point. The EDS valuations associated with this elliptic curve are given by

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=R_{m}(k,2k+1)=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k(k+1)}{n}m^{2}-\frac{\overline{mk}_{n}(n-\overline{mk}_{n})}{n}}}\right)\,.

(148)

For the codimension-two behavior, we need to find the EDS valuations
v(W_{m}^{(2)})
corresponding to
\text{I}^{*}_{n-4}
loci with at
\{\sigma=b_{1}=0\}
with
\mu=1
or
3
. The fibers at
\text{I}^{*}
loci have cusp singularities, so the analogous elliptic curves should have additive reduction, unlike those with multiplicative reduction we encountered previously.
Fortunately,
StangeEllTrouble
gives formulas for the valuations of EDSs for elliptic curves of additive reduction. The key insight is that, after a field extension by an element
p^{\prime}
, elliptic curves with additive reduction can be converted to a more minimal form with either good or multiplicative reduction. As an example, consider an elliptic curve
E
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of the form

y^{2}=x^{3}+p^{2}f^{\prime}x+p^{3}g^{\prime}\,,

(149)

where
p
is a prime integer and
f^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}
are integers not proportional to
p
that satisfy

\Delta=4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{2}f^{\prime}}}\right)^{3}+27\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{3}g^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\propto p^{6+(n-4)}

(150)

for an integer
n\geq 4
. This elliptic curve, the analogue of
\text{I}^{*}_{n-4}
singular fibers, has additive reduction modulo
p
. Suppose we extend
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
by
p^{\prime}=\sqrt{p}
, such that the elliptic curve can be written as

y^{2}=x^{3}+(p^{\prime})^{4}f^{\prime}x+(p^{\prime})^{6}g^{\prime}\,.

(151)

The elliptic curve is now in a non-minimal form, and we can remove the factors of
{p^{\prime}}
by defining

x=(p^{\prime})^{2}x^{\prime}\,,\quad y=(p^{\prime})^{3}y^{\prime}\,.

(152)

After plugging in these redefinitions and removing the common factor of
(p^{\prime})^{6}
, we obtain a new elliptic curve
E^{\prime}
of the form

{y^{\prime}}^{2}={x^{\prime}}^{3}+f^{\prime}x^{\prime}+g^{\prime}\,.

(153)

with a discriminant

\Delta_{E^{\prime}}=\frac{\Delta_{E}}{(p^{\prime})^{12}}\,.

(154)

E^{\prime}
no longer has additive reduction modulo
p^{\prime}
. If
E^{\prime}
has good reduction, we say that the original curve
E
has potential good reduction. If
E^{\prime}
has multiplicative reduction, the original curve
E
has potential multiplicative reduction.

Now consider the elliptic divisibility sequence
W_{m}
associated with a point
P
on
E
. The point
P
maps onto a point
P^{\prime}
on
E^{\prime}
, and we can form an EDS
W_{m}^{\prime}
associated with
P^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
. We can also have a valuation
v_{1}
for the extended field that is essentially a valuation with respect to
p^{\prime}
: for an element
\zeta
of the original field,
v_{1}(\zeta)=dv(\zeta)
, where
d
is the ramification degree of the extension. For the example in
Eq.

149
, the ramification degree is
2
because we extend the field by
\sqrt{p}
.

The valuations
v(W_{m})
can be related to the valuations
v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})
. According to Theorem 19 of
StangeEllTrouble
, if
E
has potential good reduction, the valuations of
W_{m}
are given by the formula

dv(W_{m})=\frac{m^{2}-1}{12}dv(\Delta_{E})+v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})\,.

(155)

And according to Theorem 14 of
StangeEllTrouble
, the valuations
v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})
take the form

v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\frac{v_{1}(x^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}})}{2}m^{2}&n_{P^{\prime}}=1\\
0&n_{P^{\prime}}\neq 1\end{cases}+\begin{cases}S_{m/n_{{P^{\prime}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime},b,v_{1}(p^{\prime}),h,s,w}}\right)&m\mid n_{{P^{\prime}}}\\
0&m\nmid n_{P^{\prime}}\end{cases}\,.

(156)

Here,
x^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}}
is the
x^{\prime}
coordinate of
P^{\prime}
, and
n_{P^{\prime}}
is the smallest integer such that
n_{P^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
reduces to the identity of
E^{\prime}
modulo
p^{\prime}
. The
S
term in described in more detail in
Appendix

B
, but as argued there it can essentially be replaced by the constant
s
for the cases of interest in this paper.

If
E
has potential multiplicative reduction, Theorem 29 of
StangeEllTrouble
states that the valuations of
W_{m}
are given by

dv(W_{m})=\frac{m^{2}-1}{4}dv(f)+v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})\,,

(157)

where
f
can be read off from the equation for
E
.
30
30

30

If
E
is written in Tate form,
f
takes the standard form
-((a_{1}^{2}+4a_{2})^{2}-24(a_{1}a_{3}+a_{4}))/48
. The quantity we refer to as
f
would more commonly be referred to as
c_{4}(E)
in the mathematics literature.
If
P^{\prime}
reduces to a point other than the singular point, the
v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})
are still given by
Eq.

156
. However, if
P^{\prime}
reduces to the singular point, the
v_{1}(W_{m}^{\prime})
are given by the multiplicative reduction expressions, and the
v(W_{m})
valuations take the form

v(W_{m})=\frac{m^{2}-1}{4}v(f)+\frac{1}{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{m}(a,l)+\begin{cases}S_{m/n_{P^{\prime}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime},p^{\prime},v_{1}(p^{\prime}),0,s,w}}\right)&n_{P^{\prime}}\mid m\\
0&n_{P^{\prime}}\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)

(158)

for integers
a
,
l
, and
n_{P^{\prime}}
.

To apply this to the codimension-two
\text{I}^{*}_{n-4}
singularities, we consider an elliptic curve found by taking
Eq.

124
, setting
\sigma
and
b_{1}
to integers
p
and
6p
(where
p
is a prime integer not equal to 2 or 3), and letting other parameters be appropriate numbers. For a particular choice of these numbers, we obtain the elliptic curve
E

y^{2}=x^{3}+p^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-3(1-3p)^{2}+4p^{k-1}}}\right)x+p^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2(3p-1)^{3}-4(3p-1)p^{k-1}+p^{2k-3}}}\right)\,,

(159)

which admits a rational point

P\colon(x,y)=(p(3p-1),p^{k})\,.

(160)

This elliptic curve has additive reduction with
v(\Delta)=2k+3
. After following the procedure outlined above, the resulting elliptic curve
E^{\prime}
has multiplicative reduction modulo
p^{\prime}
with
l=v_{1}(\Delta^{\prime})=4k-6
. Additionally,
P^{\prime}
reduces to the singular point on
E^{\prime}
. However,
4P^{\prime}
reduces to the identity point, implying that
n_{P^{\prime}}=4
. The
y^{\prime}
coordinate of
P^{\prime}
is proportional to
{p^{\prime}}^{2k-3}
, and
a
should be
2k-3
. Therefore, the EDS valuations for the codimension-two behavior are given by

\displaystyle v(W_{m}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}-1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{m}(2k-3,4k-6)+\begin{cases}S_{m/4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime},p^{\prime},v_{1}(p^{\prime}),0,s,w}}\right)&4\mid m\\
0&4\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)

(161)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}1-s&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{n}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}\,.

Combining everything together, we expect that when the antisymmetric matter has charge or pseudo-charge
q=\pm\frac{m}{n}
,
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
should be given by

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{8n}m^{2}+\frac{\overline{mk}_{n}(n-\overline{mk}_{n})}{2n}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}1-s&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{n}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}\,.

(162)

Demanding that this formula give the observed
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
of 1 for antisymmetric matter of
\mathfrak{su}(5)
with
m=4
, we can set
s
to 1, and if we plug in

m=nq\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m(k+1)}_{n}=n-\overline{mk}_{n}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{m}_{4}=\overline{nq}_{4}\,,

(163)

we find that

\displaystyle v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{4}q^{2}+\frac{\nu(n-\nu)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\mu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)

(164)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{SU(n)}}{d_{SO(2n)}}q^{2}+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(2n)}(\mu)}}\right)\,,

in exact agreement with the proposals in
Section

2.2
and the observed order-of-vanishing data.

Antisymmetric representation of
\mathfrak{su}(n)
for even
n

For the antisymmetric representation with even
n
(i.e,
n=2k
), we obtained the order-of-vanishing data from two types of seed models admitting
\bm{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}_{2/n}
matter: those with
\nu=1
and those with
\nu=\frac{n}{2}+1
. In order to establish a connection to the EDS valuations, we need to analyze each seed model separately. Still, we can see that the formulas from
StangeEllTrouble
reproduce the observed order of vanishing data and the expressions expected from the proposals.

We begin with the
\nu=1
seed construction, which is described by the the Weierstrass model in
Eq.

113
with the substitutions in
Eq.

115
. The model’s
\mathfrak{su}(2k)
gauge factor is supported along the codimension-one locus
\{\sigma=0\}
, which supports
\text{I}_{2k}^{(s)}
singularities. The
\bm{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}_{2/n}
matter is supported at the codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=b_{1,0}=0\}
. As before, we need EDS valuations corresponding to both the codimension-one and the codimension-two behavior. The codimension-one EDS valuations, which were calculated above, are given by

v(W^{(1)}_{m})=R_{m}(1,n)=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n-1}{n}m^{2}-\frac{\overline{m}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m}_{n}}}\right)}{n}}}\right)\,.

(165)

We can calculate the codimension-two EDS valuations through a procedure similar to that used for antisymmetric matter with odd
n
. This analysis is performed in
Section

C.1.2
, so as not to clutter the discussion here. The end result is that

v(W^{(2)}_{m})=\frac{1}{2}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&m\mid 2\\
1&m\nmid 2\end{cases}\,.

(166)

Combining these two expressions we find that

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}m^{2}+\frac{\overline{m}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m}_{n}}}\right)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
1&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(167)

If we use the relations

q=\frac{2}{n}m\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m}_{n}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-\frac{2\nu}{n}}}\right)}_{2}+\overline{2\nu}_{4}=2\overline{m}_{2}

(168)

for this seed model, our result can be rewritten as

\displaystyle v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{4}q^{2}+\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\nu}}\right)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\end{cases}}}\right)

(169)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{\SU(n)}}{d_{\SO(2n)}}q^{2}+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(n)}(\nu)}}\right)\,.

The
\nu=\frac{n}{2}+1
seed construction is described by the Weierstrass model in
Eq.

132
. The model supports
\text{I}^{(s)}_{2k}
singularities along
\{\sigma=0\}
and a generating section with components given by
Eq.

133
. The
\bm{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}_{2/n}
matter is supported at the codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=b_{1}=0\}
. To find the EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-one behavior, we can construct an elliptic curve analogue by setting
\sigma
to some prime
p
and the other parameters to some arbitrary numbers. The resulting elliptic curve would have multiplicative reduction modulo
p
with
v_{p}(\Delta)=2k
, and the codimension-one EDS valuations should be given by

v(W^{(1)}_{m})=R_{m}(k+1,2k)=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(k+1)(k-1)}{2k}m^{2}-\frac{\overline{m(k+1)}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m(k+1)}_{n}}}\right)}{n}}}\right)\,.

(170)

Calculating the codimension-two EDS valuations requires using the procedure for elliptic curves with additive reduction. The analysis is performed in
Section

C.1.1
, and the end result is

v(W^{(2)}_{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{4}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{n}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(171)

Combining the results, we find that

v(W^{(2)}_{m})-v(W^{(1)}_{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}m^{2}+\frac{\overline{m(k+1)}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{m(k+1)}_{n}}}\right)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{n}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(172)

If we use the relations

q=\frac{2}{n}m\,,\quad\nu=\overline{(k+1)m}_{n}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-\frac{2\nu}{n}}}\right)}_{2}+\overline{2\nu}_{4}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\overline{(k+1)}_{2}}}\right)\overline{m}_{2}\,,

(173)

for this seed model, our result can be rewritten as

\displaystyle v(W^{(2)}_{m})-v(W^{(1)}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}q^{2}+\frac{\overline{\nu}_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\overline{\nu}_{n}}}\right)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
\frac{n}{4}&\mu=1\text{ or }3\end{cases}

(174)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{\SU(n)}}{d_{\SO(2n)}}q^{2}+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(n)}(\nu)}}\right).

To summarize, we see that, regardless of which seed model for
\mathfrak{su}(2k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
antisymmetric matter we use, the expressions for
v(W^{(2)}_{m})-v(W^{(1)}_{m})
calculated using the formulas from
StangeEllTrouble
agree with the proposals in
Section

2.2
. Moreover, these formulas reproduce the orders of vanishing observed by calculating multiples of the generating sections. This result gives us further confidence in the proposals.

8
Representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

We now turn to models with
\mathfrak{so}(2n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebras, focusing on matter charged in the vector and spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
. Both these matter representations occur at codimension-two loci where a codimension-one locus with
\text{I}^{*s}_{n-4}
singular fibers intersects the residual
\text{I}_{1}
discriminant locus. The ideas and strategies used for the singlet and
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
situations carry over here, but the analysis is more challenging due to the centers of the universal covering groups.
\Spin(4k)
, the universal covering group of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
, has a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
center, but
\Spin(4k+2)
, the universal covering group of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
, has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
center. We therefore analyze the
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
and
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
representations separately. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
center poses the additional complication that it has more than one generating element, as seen above when investigating the antisymmetric representation of
\mathfrak{su}(n)
. We therefore need a larger number of seed models to generate a representative sample of pseudo-charges.

8.1
Spinor representations
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)

We begin with matter charged in the spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
, the simplest of the
\mathfrak{so}(2n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
situations considered here. As discussed in
Section

4.2
, the two spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
are conjugate to each other, so a codimension-two locus supporting
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
spinor matter supports fields in both representations. Here, we restrict our attention to the spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(10)
and
\mathfrak{so}(14)
, as attempts to realize spinor matter in F-theory for
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
algebras beyond
\mathfrak{so}(14)
produce constructions with codimension-two loci where
f
and
g
vanish to orders
(4,6)
. The spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(10)
occur at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from type
\text{I}^{*s}_{1}
to type
\text{IV}^{*}
; in terms of the ADE groups, this is an enhancement from
\Spin(10)
to
\gE_{6}
. The spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(14)
meanwhile occur at codimension-two loci with an
\text{I}^{*s}_{3}\to\text{II}^{*}
(or
\Spin(14)\rightarrow\gE_{8}
) enhancement.

Both
\Spin(10)
and
\Spin(14)
have
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
centers, and
\nu
can take a value from
0
to
3
. For
\mathfrak{so}(10)
, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for the spinor with highest weight
[0,0,0,0,1]
are
\frac{4-\nu}{4}+\mathbb{Z}
. For
\mathfrak{so}(14)
, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for the spinor with highest weight
[0,0,0,0,0,0,1]
are
\frac{\nu}{4}+\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose we have a seed
\mathfrak{so}(10)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
or
\mathfrak{so}(14)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model with a codimension-two locus supporting spinor matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
\pm\frac{1}{4}
. This model would have either
\nu=1
or
\nu=3
, and by the arguments in
Section

5.1
, we can freely choose our generating section such that we obtain either value of
\nu
. Then, we can find multiples
m\hat{s}
of the generating section that would realize pseudo-charge
\pm\frac{m}{4}
at the spinor locus with either
\nu=\overline{m}_{4}
(if the chosen generating section has
\nu=1
) or
\nu=\overline{3m}_{4}
(if the chosen generating section has
\nu=3
). We can therefore generate all the allowed combinations of pseudo-charge and
\nu
from this seed model. Since the orders of vanishing of the section components are insensitive to the sign of the pseudo-charge, the orders of vanishing for pseudo-charge
-q
are the same as those for pseudo-charge
+q
. Based on the discussion in
Section

5
, we can use these data to establish formulas relating the orders of vanishing to the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the spinor matter.

\mathfrak{so}(10)

We consider the Weierstrass model given by

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{1}{3}\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,1}^{2}+3\sigma c_{0,3}}}\right)+\sigma b_{2,1}b_{0,0}c_{2,2}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{1,2}c_{3,1}}}\right)}}\right)xz^{4}

(175)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{108}\Big[\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(27c_{3,1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,1}^{2}+4\sigma c_{0,3}}}\right)+8\sigma^{2}c_{2,2}^{3}-36\sigma c_{1,2}c_{3,1}c_{2,2}}}\right)

\displaystyle-4b_{0,0}^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(9\sigma b_{2,1}c_{0,3}+2b_{2,1}^{3}}}\right)+3\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(9c_{1,2}^{2}-4c_{2,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,1}^{2}+6\sigma c_{0,3}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle+6\sigma b_{2,1}b_{0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\sigma c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{1,2}c_{3,1}}}\right)\Big]z^{6}\,.

This model is equivalent to the
\mathcal{Q}(3,2,2,1,0,1,1)
model from
KuntzlerTateTrees
, which has an
\mathfrak{so}(10)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra. The
\mathfrak{so}(10)
algebra is tuned along
\{\sigma=0\}
, while the Weierstrass model admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}c_{3,1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}b_{2,1}+2\sigma c_{2,2}}}\right)\,,

(176)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{2}c_{3,1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}b_{2,1}+2\sigma c_{2,2}}}\right)-c_{1,2}b_{0,0}^{4}-2\sigma c_{3,1}^{3}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=b_{0,0}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{3}\Big[b_{0,0}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{2,2}^{2}+c_{1,2}c_{3,1}}}\right)-4\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}^{2}-c_{0,3}b_{0,0}^{6}

\displaystyle+b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{5}c_{2,2}-2b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{3}c_{3,1}^{2}+3\sigma c_{3,1}^{4}\Big]\,.

As can be verified by comparison with the listed matter spectrum in
KuntzlerTateTrees
, this model supports
\bm{16}_{-\frac{1}{4}}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
,
\bm{16}_{\frac{3}{4}}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
, and
\bm{10}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}c_{1,2}-b_{2,1}c_{3,1}=0\}
. For this model,
\nu
is
1
. (Alternatively, we can flip the signs of the
\bm{16}
charges and take
\nu
to be
3
; since the
\bm{10}
representation is real, the sign of its charge is irrelevant as per the arguments in
Section

5.1
.) After determining the spectrum of charged singlets and accounting for nonlocal adjoint matter uncharged under the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebra, one can verify that this matter spectrum satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}+2[b_{0,0}]-\frac{5}{4}[\sigma]
.

We now calculate multiples of the generating section
\hat{s}
, examine their behavior at the
\bm{16}_{-\frac{1}{4}}
locus
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
, and find the orders of vanishing corresponding to various pseudo-charges. We can also find the orders of vanishing at the
\bm{16}_{\frac{3}{4}}
locus, providing both an additional check on the data from the
\bm{16}_{-\frac{1}{4}}
locus and the ability to probe larger pseudo-charges without calculating larger multiples of
\hat{s}
. The resulting data are listed in
Table

14(a)
. The observed codimension-one orders of vanishing satisfy the equations

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(10)}(\nu)\,,

(177)

And if we let

\mu=\overline{4q}_{3}\,,

(178)

where
q
is the pseudo-charge, the codimension-two orders of vanishing satisfy the equations

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{3}q^{2}+T_{\Spin(10)}(\nu)-T_{\gE_{6}}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

(179)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=(2,3,4)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{6}}(\mu)\,.

(180)

\Spin(10)

\bm{64}_{q}\oplus\bm{14}_{2q}

Table 14
:
Orders of vanishing for various combinations of
\nu
and pseudo-charge
q
and for spinor matter in
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models. The
\mathfrak{so}(10)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
data are found using the model in
Eq.

175
, while the
\mathfrak{so}(14)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
data are found using the model in
Eq.

183
.

\mathfrak{so}(14)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

Matter charged in the spinor (
\bm{64}
) representation of
\mathfrak{so}(14)
occurs at loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}_{3}^{*s}
(or
\Spin(14)
) to
\text{II}^{*}
(or
\gE_{8}
). These codimension-two loci also support matter in the vector
(\bm{14})
representation. In a 6D model, for instance, an irreducible codimension-two
\text{I}_{3}^{*s}\to\text{II}^{*}
locus supports a hypermultiplet of
\bm{64}
matter and a hypermultiplet of
\bm{14}
matter. We should therefore determine how the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the
\bm{64}
matter compares with that of the
\bm{14}
matter at the same locus. The
\bm{14}
representation has a weight
[0,0,0,0,0,-1,1]
, and the
\bm{64}
representation has weights
[0,0,0,0,0,0,1]
and
[0,0,0,0,0,-1,0]
.
31
31

31

We follow the notation of
Slansky
where the
\bm{64}
representation has highest weight
[0,0,0,0,0,0,1]
. Other sources may use alternative conventions.
If the curves in the
\gE_{8}
fiber supporting these two
\bm{64}
weights are
c_{+}
and
c_{-}
, then one can verify that
c_{+}+c_{-}
is one of the two curves in the fiber supporting the
\bm{14}
weight.
32
32

32

Because the
\gE_{8}
adjoint representation decomposes as
\bm{91}\oplus\bm{1}\oplus\bm{64}\oplus\overline{\bm{64}}\oplus\bm{14}\oplus\bm{14}
, there are two curves corresponding to the
[0,0,0,0,0,-1,1]
weight of the
\bm{14}
representation. We only need one of them for the purposes of this argument.
All the weights in the
\bm{64}
representation should have the same
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q
, implying that, for a section
\hat{s}
,

\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot c_{+}=\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot c_{-}=q\,.

(181)

But the curve
c_{+}+c_{-}
should then satisfy

\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{+}+c_{-}}}\right)=2q\,.

(182)

The charge of the
[0,0,0,0,0,-1,1]
weight should be the same as the charge of the other weights in the same
\bm{14}
representation. Therefore, the charge of the
\bm{14}
matter should be twice that of the
\bm{64}
matter. In other words, the codimension-two locus should support matter fields in the
\bm{64}_{q}
and
\bm{14}_{2q}
representations, which are accompanied by fields in the
\overline{\bm{64}}_{-q}
and
\bm{14}_{-2q}
representations. In a 6D model, an irreducible
\text{I}_{3}^{*s}\to\text{II}^{*}
locus would support a
\bm{64}_{q}
hypermultiplet and and a
\bm{14}_{2q}
hypermultiplet. When we say that an
\text{I}_{3}^{*s}\to\text{II}^{*}
locus supports matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q
, we implicitly take
q
to be the charge of the spinor matter; the charge of the vector matter can automatically be determined from this information.

We now consider the Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{3}\Big[4\beta_{2,1}^{2}b_{0,0}^{8}+8\sigma\beta_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{4}c_{2,2}

(183)

\displaystyle+3\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{0,4}-4\beta_{2,1}c_{3,1}^{2}}}\right)+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{1,3}c_{3,1}}}\right)\Big]xz^{4}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{108}\Big[-64\beta_{2,1}^{3}b_{0,0}^{12}-24\sigma^{2}\beta_{2,1}\Big(3b_{0,0}^{6}c_{0,4}

\displaystyle+b_{0,0}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(5c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{1,3}c_{3,1}}}\right)-12b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}^{2}+9c_{3,1}^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle+96\sigma\beta_{2,1}^{2}b_{0,0}^{6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3c_{3,1}^{2}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}}}\right)+\sigma^{3}\Big(9b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3c_{1,3}^{2}-8c_{0,4}c_{2,2}}}\right)

\displaystyle+4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2c_{2,2}^{3}-9c_{1,3}c_{3,1}c_{2,2}+27c_{0,4}c_{3,1}^{2}}}\right)\Big)\Big]z^{6}\,.

The
(f,g,\Delta)
of this model vanish to orders
(2,3,9)
at
\{\sigma=0\}
, and the split condition for
\text{I}_{3}^{*}

KatzEtAlTate
;
GrassiMorrison
;
MorrisonTaylorClusters
singularities is satisfied. The model therefore supports an
\mathfrak{so}(14)
gauge algebra along
\{\sigma=0\}
. It also admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\beta_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{6}+2\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}-3\sigma c_{3,1}^{2}}}\right)\,,

(184)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{4}c_{1,3}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}+2c_{3,1}^{3}}}\right)\,,

(185)

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=b_{0,0}\,,

(186)

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-b_{0,0}^{6}c_{0,4}+b_{0,0}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{2,2}^{2}+c_{1,3}c_{3,1}}}\right)-4b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}^{2}+3c_{3,1}^{4}}}\right)\,,

(187)

indicating that the full gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{so}(14)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. At
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
, the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}_{3}^{*s}
to
\text{II}^{*}
(or from
\Spin(14)
to
\gE_{8}
). This codimension-two locus supports
\bm{64}_{\frac{1}{4}}\oplus\bm{14}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, along with the appropriate conjugate matter.
At
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}^{4}c_{1,3}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}+2c_{3,1}^{3}=0\}
, the singularity type enhances to
\text{I}_{4}^{*}
(or
\Spin(16)
), and the locus supports
\bm{14}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter. Based on these
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges,
\nu
is
1
. (Alternatively, we could say that the model has
\nu=3
with
\bm{64}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\oplus\bm{14}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
. Because the
\bm{14}
representation is real, the sign of its
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge is not important as per the arguments in
Section

5.1
.) While
(f,g,\Delta)
vanish to orders
(4,6,12)
at
\{\sigma=\beta_{2,1}=0\}
, the presence of such loci can be avoided by an appropriate choice of divisor classes. For instance, if we take
[\beta_{2,1}]
to be trivial, there are no codimension-two
(4,6,12)
loci. After calculating the spectrum of charged singlets and
\mathfrak{so}(14)
adjoints uncharged under the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebra, one can show that this spectrum satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions, at least for situations without codimension-two
(4,6,12)
loci.

As before, we can calculate multiples of
\hat{s}
and find the orders of vanishing of the section components at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
. These numbers are listed in
Table

14(b)
along with the corresponding values of
\nu
and of the spinor pseudo-charge
q
. The codimension-one orders of vanishing satisfy the equations

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(14)}(\nu)\,.

(188)

The codimension-two orders of vanishing, meanwhile, satisfy the equations

\displaystyle\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4q^{2}+T_{\Spin(14)}(\nu)}}\right)\,,

(189)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=(2,3,4)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})\,.

(190)

The center for
\gE_{8}
, the group associated with the codimension-two singularity type, is the trivial group. As such, the only allowed value of
\mu
is 0, and
T_{\gE_{8}}(\mu)
and
\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{8}}(\mu)
are both trivial. Keeping this in mind, we see that the observed orders of vanishing for the
\mathfrak{so}(14)
spinors agree with the proposals in
Section

2.2
.

8.2
Spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)

We now turn to the spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
. We restrict our attention to the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
and
\mathfrak{so}(12)
, as attempts to obtain spinors for larger values of
k
produce models with codimension-two loci where
(f,g)
vanish to orders
(4,6)
. Both of these algebras have two spinor representations. For
\mathfrak{so}(8)
, the two spinor representations are
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
, with highest weight
[0,0,0,1]
, and
\bm{8}_{c}
, with highest weight
[0,0,1,0]
. Since these two representations are both real, they occur at different codimension-two loci in the F-theory model. For
\mathfrak{so}(12)
, the two spinor representations are
\bm{32}
, with highest weight
[0,0,0,0,0,1]
, and
\bm{32^{\prime}}
, with highest weight
[0,0,0,0,1,0]
.
33
33

33

While this convention agrees with
YamatsuGroupTheory
, the highest weights of the
\bm{32}
and
\bm{32^{\prime}}
representation are switched in the conventions of
Slansky
.
They are pseudoreal representations that occur at distinct codimension-two loci. While the
\bm{32}
and
\bm{32^{\prime}}
representations can in principle come in half-hypermultiplets in 6D models, we are most interested in situations where matter in these representations also has a nonzero charge under an
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebra. The total representation is no longer pseudoreal after including this charge, so
\mathfrak{so}(12)
spinor matter with a nonzero
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge must occur in full hypermultiplets. Because both the
\mathfrak{so}(8)
and
\mathfrak{so}(12)
spinor representations are self-conjugate, the sign of the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge is unimportant for these cases.

The universal covering groups
\Spin(8)
and
\Spin(12)
both have
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
centers. As discussed in
Section

4
, we label the four elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
with an integer
\nu
ranging from 0 to 3. The allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges are integral for all the spinor representations when
\nu=0
and are half-integral for all the spinor representations when
\nu=2
. When
\nu=1
, the allowed charges for
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
and
\bm{32^{\prime}}
representations are half-integral, while those for the
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}
and
\bm{32}
representations are integral. When
\nu=3
, the roles are reversed: allowed charges for
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}
and
\bm{32}
representations are half-integral, while those for the
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
and
\bm{32}^{\prime}
representations are integral. Note that the
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
fibers admit an automorphism that exchanges the two nodes at the end of the Dynkin diagram; these are the two nodes on the right hand side in
Fig.

3
. This automorphism exchanges the two spinor representations for a given algebra. In turn, the automorphism sends
\nu=1
to
\nu=3
, and vice versa. The elements
\nu=2
and
\nu=0
are sent to themselves under the automorphism. In F-theory models, there is an ambiguity in the labeling of the curves exchanged under the automorphism, so we can perform the automorphism without changing the underlying geometry. As a result, we can often determine geometric information about one spinor representation from information about the other spinor representation. For instance, if we knew the orders of vanishing for
\bm{32}
matter with some
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge when
\nu=1
, we automatically know the orders of vanishing for
\bm{32}^{\prime}
matter with the same charge when
\nu=3
.

With this in mind, we can now describe how to generate various combinations of the pseudo-charge and
\nu
. Starting with a
\nu=1
model with
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,\frac{1}{2}}
or
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, we can obtain all the half-integral
\nu=1
pseudo-charges and all the integral
\nu=0
pseudo-charges. If we start with a
\nu=2
model with
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,\frac{1}{2}}
or
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, we can obtain all the half-integral
\nu=2
pseudo-charges while reproducing all the integral
\nu=0
pseudo-charges. Obtaining the integral
\nu=3
pseudo-charges is more difficult. If we have a
\nu=3
model with
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,1}
or
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{1}
matter, we can generate the odd
\nu=3
pseudo-charges but not the even ones: the even multiples of the generating section, which would realize the even pseudo-charges, would have
\nu=0
according to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group structure. We could generate some of the even
\nu=3
pseudo-charges from a model with
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,2}
or
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{2}
matter, but we would miss those pseudo-charges that are multiples of 4. Even if we attempt to fill in these gaps by starting with charges that are larger and larger powers of
2
, we always miss some of the even
\nu=3
pseudo-charges. We are therefore unable to practically obtain order-of-vanishing data for all of the even
\nu=3
pseudo-charges in a particular range. Nevertheless, we find that the order-of-vanishing data we are able to obtain follow the expected formulas in
Section

2.2
, making it highly plausible that the missing pseudo-charges follow these same patterns. Of course, once we have data for the
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
and
\bm{32^{\prime}}
representations, we can use the automorphism to automatically obtain data for the
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}
and
\bm{32}
representations. This allows us to cut down on the number of seed models used. In fact, the triality of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
models, discussed in more detail below, implies that we can read off orders of vanishing for the vector representation from the spinor data, and vice versa. As a result, we can obtain a large portion of the
\mathfrak{so}(8)
data from just a single model, and the discussion of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
spinors will also cover the
\bm{8_{\text{v}}}
representation.

\mathfrak{so}(8)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

The two spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
and the vector representation
\bm{8_{\text{v}}}
all occur at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}_{0}^{*s}
(
\Spin(8)
) to
\text{I}_{1}^{*}
(
\Spin(10)
), a fact that reflects the triality of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
. As can be seen from the symmetries of its Dynkin diagram, the
\mathfrak{so}(8)
algebra admits automorphisms that exchange the
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
,
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}
, and the
\bm{8_{\text{v}}}
representations. These automorphisms also exchange the
\nu=1
,
\nu=2
, and
\nu=3
elements of the center in a way consistent with the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges in
Table

6
. For example,
{\bm{8_{\text{c}}}}_{,1}
matter (with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q=1
) in a
\nu=1
model can alternatively be viewed as
{\bm{8_{\text{v}}}}_{,1}
matter in a
\nu=2
model or as
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,1}
matter in a
\nu=3
model.

Let us consider the Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}+\frac{1}{3}\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-3b_{0,0}^{2}b_{2,1}^{2}+3\sigma c_{1,2}c_{3,1}-c_{2,1}^{2}}}\right)xz^{4}

(191)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{108}\sigma^{3}\Big(27\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{1,2}^{2}+108\sigma b_{2,1}^{2}c_{3,1}^{2}-72b_{0,0}^{2}b_{2,1}^{2}c_{2,1}

\displaystyle-36\sigma c_{1,2}c_{3,1}c_{2,1}+8c_{2,1}^{3}\Big)z^{6}\,.

The elliptic fibration has
\text{I}_{0}^{*s}
singularities along
\{\sigma=0\}
, signaling the presence of an
\mathfrak{so}(8)
algebra. It also admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

[\hat{x}:\hat{y}:\hat{z}]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{3}\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\sigma c_{3,1}^{2}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}}}\right):-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{4}c_{1,2}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}c_{3,1}+2\sigma c_{3,1}^{3}}}\right):b_{0,0}}}\right]

(192)

and

\hat{w}=\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{1,2}c_{3,1}+c_{2,1}^{2}}}\right)-4\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}c_{3,1}^{2}-b_{2,1}^{2}b_{0,0}^{6}+3\sigma^{2}c_{3,1}^{4}}}\right)\,.

(193)

Thus the total gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{so}(8)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. There are four codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances to
\text{I}_{1}^{*}
:
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
,
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
,
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}-b_{0,0}b_{2,1}=0\}
, and
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}+b_{0,0}b_{2,1}=0\}
. The locus
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
supports matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q=1
, the locus
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
supports matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q=0
, and the remaining two loci support matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q=\frac{1}{2}
. Because of the triality of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
, there are multiple ways of describing the
\mathfrak{so}(8)
representations supported at these loci, which are summarized in
Table

15
. Regardless of which description of the matter representations is used, the spectrum satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}+2[b_{0,0}]-[\sigma]
.

To generate the order-of-vanishing data, we calculate multiples of
\hat{s}
and find the orders of vanishing of the section components at the various loci. The results are listed in
Table

16
. Note that these results do not include orders of vanishing for some combinations of
\nu
and pseudo-charge. For instance, we have only given orders of vanishing for the odd integer pseudo-charges when
\nu=1,2,3
in light of the difficulties in generating the even integer pseudo-charges described above. We have also included only the even integer pseudo-charges for
\nu=0
when
\lvert q\rvert>8
; this simply reflects the data we chose to generate, and there is no fundamental difficulty in obtaining the odd pseudo-charges for
\nu=0
. Despite these missing pseudo-charge values, one can see that the observed data follow the patterns proposed in
Section

2.2
. The codimension-one orders of vanishing follow the pattern

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(8)}(\nu)\,,

(194)

as expected, and if we let

\mu=\begin{cases}\overline{(2q-2\nu)}_{4}&\text{for }\bm{8_{\text{s}}}\text{ or }\bm{8_{\text{c}}}\\
\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}&\text{for }\bm{8_{\text{v}}}\end{cases}\,,

(195)

the codimension-two orders of vanishing satisfy the expressions

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\Spin(8)}(\nu)-T_{\Spin(10)}(\mu)}}\right)

(196)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(10)}(\mu)\,.

(197)

Since
d_{\Spin(8)}=d_{\Spin(10)}=4
, the codimension-two formulas agree with the expectations from
Section

2.2
. Although we did not find orders of vanishing for the even pseudo-charges when
\nu\neq 0
,
Eq.

196
is guaranteed to give us integer
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
for these cases. The expressions in
Eq.

195
give
\mu=2
whenever
q
is even and
\nu
takes the appropriate nonzero value, and plugging this information into
Eq.

196
always results in an integer. These facts suggest that the expressions in
Section

2.2
correctly relate the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
spinor or vector matter to the orders of vanishing of the section components.

\mathfrak{so}(12)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

The two spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(12)
both occur at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}_{2}^{*s}
(
\Spin(12)
) to
\text{III}^{*}
(
\gE_{7}
). After resolution, the codimension-two singularities can either be incompletely or completely resolved
MorrisonTaylorMaS
;
KanHalfHyper
. In 6D models, an incomplete resolution locus supports a half-hypermultiplet of spinor matter. A complete resolution locus supports a full hypermultiplet of spinor matter and an additional singlet, as can be seen from the decomposition of the
\gE_{7}
adjoint representation:

\displaystyle\gE_{7}

\displaystyle\to\SO(12)\times\SU(2)\,,

(198)

\displaystyle\bm{133}

\displaystyle\to(\bm{66},\bm{1})\oplus(\bm{32},\bm{2})\oplus(\bm{1},\bm{3})\,.

The
(\bm{32},\bm{2})
degrees of freedom combine to form a
\bm{32}
hypermultiplet, while the positive and negative roots of the
(\bm{1},\bm{3})
degrees of freedom form the extra singlet hypermultiplet. By the automorphism of
\mathfrak{so}(12)
, there is a similar decomposition for the
\bm{32}^{\prime}
, and this spinor is also accompanied by a singlet at complete resolution loci. Since spinor matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges must come in full hypermultiplets, we are primarily interested in the complete resolution loci. However, the spinor and singlet matter at these loci can both be charged under the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebra, raising the question of how the singlet charge compares to the spinor charge. The charge of the singlet matter is in fact twice that of the spinor matter.
34
34

34

Recall that, because the singlet representation is real, the sign of the singlet matter is unimportant.
Because the spinor matter occurs in full hypermultiplets, the resolved
\gE_{7}
fiber contains two curves,
\gamma_{+}
and
\gamma_{-}
, representing the two highest weights of the spinor representation in
(\bm{32},\bm{2})
(or
(\bm{32^{\prime}},\bm{2})
). These two curves would belong to the two different half-hypermultiplets that combine to form the full hypermultiplet of spinor matter. The half-hypermultiplets should have opposite
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges, implying that, for a generating section
\hat{s}
,

\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{+}=-\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{-}=q\,.

(199)

But
\gamma_{+}
and
\gamma_{-}
also serve as the two weights of the fundamental of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
. The difference
\gamma_{+}-\gamma_{-}
between these two curves is the root in the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
adjoint, and it corresponds to the curve supporting the singlet. The charge of the singlet matter should be given by

\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot(\gamma_{+}-\gamma_{-})=2q\,.

(200)

The
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the singlet matter is therefore twice that of the spinor matter, as claimed above. While we gave this argument for the
\bm{32}
spinor, the same argument carries through for the
\bm{32^{\prime}}
spinor without significant modifications.

The
\mathfrak{so}(12)
algebra does not admit the triality of the
\mathfrak{so}(8)
algebra. We would therefore need multiple Weierstrass models to obtain a representative set of
q
and
\nu
combinations, but we have not been able to obtain models that support
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{1}
matter with
\nu=3
or
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter with
\nu=2
(or the
\bm{32}
matter related to this
\bm{32^{\prime}}
matter by the automorphism). Whenever we attempt to construct such models,
(f,g,\Delta)
end up vanishing to orders
(4,6,12)
at the would-be spinor locus. However, there is the
\mathfrak{so}(12)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{1}{3}\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[3\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{0,3}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{3,1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,1}c_{3,1}+\sigma c_{1,3}}}\right)}}\right)+b_{2,1}^{2}b_{0,0}^{4}}}\right]xz^{4}

(201)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{108}\Big[9\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sigma b_{2,1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{2,2}^{2}+c_{1,3}c_{3,1}}}\right)+4b_{2,1}^{2}c_{3,1}^{2}+3\sigma^{2}c_{1,3}^{2}-8\sigma c_{0,3}c_{2,2}}}\right)

\displaystyle+4\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-9c_{3,1}c_{2,2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2b_{2,1}c_{3,1}+\sigma c_{1,3}}}\right)+2\sigma c_{2,2}^{3}+27c_{0,3}c_{3,1}^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle-36\sigma b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{4}c_{0,3}-8b_{2,1}^{3}b_{0,0}^{6}\Big]z^{6}\,,

which has
\text{I}_{2}^{*s}
fibers along
\{\sigma=0\}
and a generating section
\hat{s}
with components

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}+b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{4}-3\sigma c_{3,1}^{2}}}\right)\,,

(202)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{0,0}^{4}c_{1,3}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}+2c_{3,1}^{3}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=b_{0,0}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{3}}{3}\Big[3b_{0,0}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{3,1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma c_{1,3}-b_{2,1}c_{3,1}}}\right)+\sigma c_{2,2}^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle-12\sigma b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}^{2}+b_{0,0}^{6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4b_{2,1}c_{2,2}-3c_{0,3}}}\right)+9\sigma c_{3,1}^{4}\Big]\,.

The model, which has
\nu=1
, supports
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\bm{1}_{1}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
,
\bm{32}_{0}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
, and
\bm{12}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter at
\{\sigma=3b_{0,0}^{2}c_{0,3}-4b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}+3b_{2,1}c_{3,1}^{2}=0\}
. In 6D models,
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
supports full hypermultiplets of
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, while
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
supports half-hypermultiplets of
\bm{32}_{0}
matter. This same construction can alternatively be viewed as a
\nu=3
model with
\bm{32}_{\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\bm{1}_{1}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
,
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{0}
matter at
\{\sigma=b_{2,1}=0\}
, and
\bm{12}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter at
\{\sigma=3b_{0,0}^{2}c_{0,3}-4b_{2,1}b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,2}+3b_{2,1}c_{3,1}^{2}=0\}
. For either description, the massless charged spectrum, after accounting for additional singlets and
\mathfrak{so}(12)
adjoints, satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions with
\tilde{b}=-2K_{B}+2[b_{0,0}]-\frac{3}{2}[\sigma]
.

We can therefore find the orders of vanishing for at least some of the spinor pseudo-charges by calculating the components of multiples of
\hat{s}
and finding their orders of vanishing at
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
. The resulting data are given in
Table

17
. The codimension-one orders of vanishing satisfy the expression

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(12)}(\nu)\,.

(203)

And if we let
35
35

For all of the orders of vanishing in
Table

17
,
\mu
would be 0. While it might seem arbitrary to use this more complicated expression for
\mu
, it can be motivated by the requirement that
Eq.

205
give integer
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
for the
(q,\nu)
combinations that were not generated.

\mu=\overline{(2q+\nu)}_{2}\,,

(204)

the codimension-two orders of vanishing satisfy

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2q^{2}+T_{\Spin(12)}(\nu)-T_{\gE_{7}}(\mu)}}\right)

(205)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{7}}(\mu)\,.

(206)

Recalling that
d_{\Spin(12)}=4
and
d_{\gE_{7}}=2
, we see that these expressions agree exactly with those expected from
Section

2.2
. Note that we can rewrite the relationship between
q

\nu
, and
\mu
as

\mu=\begin{cases}1&\nu=2,3\\
0&\nu=0,1\end{cases}\;\text{ for }\bm{32}_{q}\oplus\bm{1}_{2q}\,,\quad\mu=\begin{cases}1&\nu=1,2\\
0&\nu=0,3\end{cases}\;\text{ for }\bm{32^{\prime}}_{q}\oplus\bm{1}_{2q}\,.

(207)

for
\mathfrak{so}(12)

\text{I}_{2}^{*s}\to\text{III}^{*}

\mathfrak{so}(12)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model in
Eq.

.

Of course, these conclusions for the
\mathfrak{so}(12)
spinors were reached with only a subset of the allowed
(q,\nu)
combinations. While we have not been able to find seed constructions for the missing combinations, it is unclear whether such models exist or whether some more fundamental constraint limits our ability to obtain these models. Perhaps some argument shows that, for instance,
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{1}
matter cannot occur in a
\nu=3
F-theory model without codimension-two (4,6,12) loci. It would be worthwhile to explore these questions in future work.

8.3
Vector representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)

We now consider the vector representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
, which occurs at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}^{*s}_{2k-3}
(or
\Spin(4k+2)
) to
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-2}
(or
\Spin(4k+4)
). Specifically, an irreducible
\text{I}^{*s}_{2k-3}\rightarrow\text{I}^{*}_{2k-2}
locus in a 6D model would support a hypermultiplet of vector (
\bm{4k+2}
) matter. As per the discussion in
Section

4.2
, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges are half-integral for
\nu=1
and
3
and integral for
\nu=0
and
2
. Since the vector representation is real, the sign of the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge is unimportant. The automorphism previously mentioned that exchanges the two spinor representations is still applicable. It sends the vector representation to itself, but as before it sends
\nu
to its inverse element in
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. Thus, the automorphism essentially has no impact on matter in the vector representation, even though it may change the value of
\nu
for the model.

We need multiple seed models to generate the pseudo-charges corresponding to the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges. If we start with a
\nu=1
model with
\bm{(4k+2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, we can generate the
\frac{1}{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
pseudo-charges for
\nu=1
, the
\frac{3}{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
pseudo-charges for
\nu=3
, the odd integral pseudo-charges for
\nu=2
, and the even integral pseudo-charges for
\nu=0
. By the automorphism, this same model also gives us the
\frac{3}{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
pseudo-charges for
\nu=1
and the
\frac{1}{2}+2\mathbb{Z}
pseudo-charges for
\nu=3
. This leaves us with the odd
\nu=0
and even
\nu=2
pseudo-charges. The odd
\nu=0
pseudo-charges can be obtained from a
\nu=0
model admitting
\bm{(4k+2)}_{1}
matter. In the process, we can also obtain the even
\nu=0
pseudo-charges, providing us an additional check on some of the data generated from the
\nu=1
model.

Obtaining the even
\nu=2
pseudo-charges is more difficult. Starting with a
\nu=2
model admitting
\bm{(4k+2)}_{2}
matter, we could obtain the even
\nu=2
pseudo-charges that are not multiples of
4
. One could use a
\nu=2
model with
\bm{(4k+2)}_{4}
vector matter to obtain some pseudo-charges that are multiples of 4, but we would still miss those pseudo-charges that are multiples of 8. In order to obtain pseudo-charges proportional to larger and larger powers of 2, we need seed models admitting vector matter with larger and larger
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges. Even obtaining a model admitting
\bm{(4k+2)}_{2}
matter is challenging, let alone models with higher charges.
36
36

36

If we take the formulas in
Section

2.2
at face value, the
\hat{z}
component of the section should vanish to order 2 at the
\bm{(4k+2)}_{2}
matter locus. The algebraic structure of such models is more complicated, since it makes use of non-UFD cancellations
Raghuram34
.
We therefore will not generate any of the even
\nu=2
pseudo-charges here.

For the
\nu=1
seed models, we use Weierstrass constructions of the form

y^{2}=x^{3}-v^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\phi^{2}+f_{k+1}v^{k-1}}}\right)xz^{4}+v^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\phi^{3}+\phi f_{k+1}v^{k-1}+\gamma^{2}v^{2k-3}}}\right)

(208)

and consider models where
k
varies from 2 to 6. To avoid the additional complications of a spinor locus, we construct these seed models over an
\mathbb{F}_{4}
base and let
\{v=0\}
be the curve of self-intersection
-4
. This model admits
\text{I}_{2k-3}^{*s}
singularities along
\{v=0\}
and a generating section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

[\hat{x}:\hat{y}:\hat{z}]=[\phi v:\gamma v^{k}:1]\,,\quad\hat{w}=-f_{k+1}v^{k+1}\,.

(209)

The gauge algebra is therefore
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. The locus
\{v=\gamma=0\}
supports
\bm{(4k+2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, while
\{f_{k+1}=\gamma=0\}
supports
\bm{1}_{1}
singlet matter. Together, this matter spectrum satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions for
0pt=-2K_{B}-\frac{2k+1}{4}[v]
. This model can be viewed as either a
\nu=1
model or as a
\nu=3
model; these two options are related by the automorphism discussed previously.

For the
\nu=0
seed models, we use Weierstrass constructions of the form

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{3}c_{2,1}^{2}+c_{3,0}\sigma^{k-1}c_{1,k+1}-\frac{1}{4}\sigma^{2k-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,k}^{2}+4c_{0,2k+1}\sigma}}\right)}}\right)xz^{4}

(210)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{108}\Big[8c_{2,1}^{3}+9\sigma^{2k-3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\sigma^{2}c_{1,k+1}^{2}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\sigma c_{2,1}-3c_{3,0}^{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,k}^{2}+4c_{0,2k+1}\sigma}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle-36c_{2,1}c_{3,0}c_{1,k+1}\sigma^{k-1}\Big]z^{6}\,,

and we let
k
vary from 2 to 6. We no longer assume that the base is
\mathbb{F}_{4}
.
37
37

37

In fact, if the base is
\mathbb{F}_{4}
and
\{\sigma=0\}
has self-intersection -4, the parameter
c_{3,0}
is forced to be proportional to
\sigma
, and the gauge algebra enhances.
Again, there are
\text{I}_{2k-3}^{*s}
singularities along
\{\sigma=0\}
, and the model admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

\displaystyle[\hat{x}:\hat{y}:\hat{z}]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[c_{3,0}^{2}-\frac{2}{3}\sigma c_{2,1}:-c_{3,0}^{3}+\sigma c_{2,1}c_{3,0}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{k+1}c_{1,k+1}:1}}\right]\,,

(211)

\displaystyle\hat{w}=3c_{3,0}^{4}-4\sigma c_{2,1}c_{3,0}^{2}+\sigma^{2}c_{2,1}^{2}+c_{3,0}\sigma^{k+1}c_{1,k+1}-\frac{1}{4}\sigma^{2k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,k}^{2}+4c_{0,2k+1}\sigma}}\right)\,.

(212)

The codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=c_{3,0}=0\}
supports
\bm{(4k+2)}_{1}
matter, while the codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=b_{2,k}=0\}
supports
\bm{(4k+2)}_{0}
matter. When
k=2
, the locus
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}=0\}
supports
\bm{16}_{0}
matter, and after including the contributions from singlets and uncharged adjoints, the spectrum satisfies the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}
. When
m>2
,
(f,g,\Delta)
vanish at least to orders
(4,6,12)
at
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}=0\}
, making it difficult to determine the matter associated with this locus and its contribution to the
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
anomaly conditions. However, if we assume that any matter contributions at
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}=0\}
are uncharged under the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
, just as was the case for
k=2
, the rest of the spectrum (including singlets) satisfies the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}
. Since we are only interested in the
\{\sigma=c_{3,0}=0\}
vector locus, we proceed with this construction in spite of the codimension-two
(4,6,12)
locus. As confirmation that this
(4,6,12)
locus does not cause significant issues, we can verify that the results for the even pseudo-charges found from the
\nu=0
construction agree with those found from the
\nu=1
construction.

We now use these constructions to generate
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models for
k
ranging from
2
to
6
and find the orders of vanishing associated with the various vector pseudo-charges. We do not list the orders of vanishing at codimension one for the sake of brevity, but they are all given by

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(4k+2)}(\nu)\,.

(213)

The orders of vanishing at codimension two are listed in
Table

18
. If we let

\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}\,,

(214)

all of these numbers follow the formulas

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\Spin(4k+2)}(\nu)-T_{\Spin(4k+4)}(\mu)}}\right)

(215)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(4k+4)}(\mu)\,.

(216)

Since the center of
\Spin(2n)
has
d_{\Spin(2n)}=4
elements, these formulas exactly agree with the expectations from
Section

2.2
.

and

.

8.4
Vector representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)

We now turn to the vector representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
. Previously, we used the triality of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
to determine the orders of vanishing for
\bm{8_{\text{v}}}
matter from those for
\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
and
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}
matter. We can no longer use this triality for
k>2
, so we must separately analyze vector matter for these cases. The vector representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
occurs at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}^{*s}_{2k-4}
(or
\Spin(4k)
) to
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-3}
(or
\Spin(4k+2)
). In 6D models, an irreducible locus of this type supports a hypermultiplet of
\bm{4k}
matter. Just as for the
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
vector representation, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for the
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
vector representation are half-integral for
\nu=1,3
and integral for
\nu=0,2
. And because the vector representation is real, the sign of the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge is unimportant for vector matter. The automorphism for
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
does not affect the vector matter but exchanges
\nu=1
and
\nu=3
.

We again need multiple models to generate a representative sample of pseudo-charges. If we start with a
\nu=1
model with
\bm{4k}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, one can obtain all the half-integral
\nu=1
pseudo-charges and all the integral
\nu=0
pseudo-charges by examining multiples of the generating section. By the automorphism, this same model can be viewed as a
\nu=3
model with
\bm{4k}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, and the
\nu=1
pseudo-charges can equivalently be viewed as
\nu=3
pseudo-charges. Therefore, we can obtain all of the half-integral
\nu=3
pseudo-charges from this same construction. This leaves us with the
\nu=2
pseudo-charges. Starting with a
\nu=2
seed model with
\bm{4k}_{1}
matter, which can be constructed relatively easily, we can get all of the odd
\nu=2
pseudo-charges. This also gives us an alternative method of generating the even
\nu=0
pseudo-charges. Obtaining the even pseudo-charges with
\nu=2
is more difficult: in order to get a pseudo-charge proportional to
2^{j}
, we would need a seed model admitting
\bm{4k}_{2^{j}}
matter. Therefore, we only generate the odd
\nu=2
pseudo-charges here.

For the
\nu=1
seed models, we consider constructions over an
\mathbb{F}_{4}
base given by the Weierstrass model

y^{2}=x^{3}-v^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\phi^{2}+f_{k}v^{k-2}}}\right)xz^{4}+v^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\phi^{3}+\phi f_{k}v^{k-2}+\gamma^{2}v^{2k-3}}}\right)\,,

(217)

where
\{v=0\}
is a curve in
\mathbb{F}_{4}
of self-intersection
-4
. We let
k
vary from 3 to 5.
38
38

38

Since we previously determined the orders of vanishing for
\mathfrak{so}(8)
vector matter, we do not need to consider the
k=2
version of this construction.
The model has
\text{I}^{*s}_{2k-4}
singular fibers along
\{v=0\}
and admits a generating section of the form

[\hat{x}:\hat{y}:\hat{z}]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[v\phi:v^{k}\gamma:1}}\right]\,,\quad\hat{w}=-v^{k}f_{k}\,,

(218)

implying that the gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{so}(4k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. The codimension-two locus
\{v=f_{k}=0\}
supports
\bm{4k}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, while the codimension-two locus
\{f_{k}=\gamma=0\}
supports
\bm{1}_{1}
matter. The resulting spectra satisfy the 6D anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}-\frac{k}{2}[v]
. By the automorphism, these constructions can alternatively be viewed as
\nu=3
model with the same matter spectra.

For the
\nu=2
seed models, we use constructions given by the Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{v^{2}}{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{2,1}^{2}+3b_{0,0}^{2}c_{0,2k-1}v^{2k-3}+3b_{0,0}^{2}b_{2,k-1}^{2}v^{2k-4}-3c_{3,1}c_{1,k}v^{k-1}}}\right)xz^{4}

(219)

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{3}}{108}\Big[-8c_{2,1}^{3}-9v^{2k-4}\Big(b_{0,0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3vc_{1,k}^{2}-8c_{2,1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,k-1}^{2}+vc_{0,2k-1}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle+12vc_{3,1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b_{2,k-1}^{2}+vc_{0,2k-1}}}\right)\Big)+36c_{2,1}c_{3,1}v^{k-1}c_{1,k}\Big]z^{6}\,.

Again, we construct these elliptic fibrations over an
\mathbb{F}_{4}
base and take
\{v=0\}
to be a curve of self-intersection
-4
. We also let
k
run from
3
to
5
. There are
\text{I}^{*s}_{2k-4}
singularities along
\{v=0\}
, and the Weierstrass model admits a generating section with components

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{v}{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3vc_{3,1}^{2}-2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}}}\right)\,,

(220)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{2}}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2b_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}c_{3,1}-b_{0,0}^{4}c_{1,k}v^{k-2}-2vc_{3,1}^{3}}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=b_{0,0}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=-v^{2}\Big(-b_{0,0}^{4}c_{2,1}^{2}+4vb_{0,0}^{2}c_{2,1}c_{3,1}^{2}-3v^{2}c_{3,1}^{4}-b_{0,0}^{4}c_{3,1}v^{k-1}c_{1,k}

\displaystyle+b_{0,0}^{6}v^{2k-4}b_{2,k-1}^{2}+b_{0,0}^{6}c_{0,2k-1}v^{2k-3}\Big)\,.

Thus, the gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{so}(4k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
.
39
39

39

For
k=5
,
[c_{0,2m-1}]
is ineffective. As a result, there is an extra generating section, and the gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{so}(20)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. However, the extra
\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge factor does not affect the discussion and can be safely ignored.
The model supports
\bm{4k}_{1}
matter at
\{v=b_{0,0}=0\}
and
\bm{4k}_{0}
matter at
\{v=b_{2,k-1}=0\}
. After accounting for charged singlets, this spectrum satisfies the anomaly cancellation conditions for
0pt=-2K_{B}+2[b_{0,0}]-[v]
.

We now construct multiples of the generating sections in these models and find the orders of vanishing for various pseudo-charges
q
of
\bm{4k}
vector matter. We do not list the codimension-one orders of vanishing here for brevity, but the generated data for
k=2
through
5
satisfy the equations

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(4k)}(\nu)\,.

(221)

The codimension-two orders of vanishing for
k=3
through

are listed in
Table

; for

\mathfrak{so}(8)

Table

\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}\,,

(222)

all of the codimension-two orders of vanishing satisfy the equations

\displaystyle\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\Spin(4k)}(\nu)-T_{\Spin(4k+2)}(\mu)}}\right)\,,

(223)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\Spin(4k+2)}(\mu)\,.

(224)

Since
\Spin(2n)
