8.5
Connection to EDS valuations

We can now show that the formulas for EDS valuations in
StangeEllTrouble
reproduce the patterns for
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
and
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
models proposed in
Section

2.2
and observed in this section. As described in
Section

7.4
, for each seed model, we should subtract the EDS valuations for the codimension-one behavior from those for the codimension-two behavior. The resulting numbers should agree with the observed
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
values for multiples of the seed model’s generating section admitting the appropriate pseudo-charges.

There are five singularity types that need to be considered for the
\mathfrak{so}
representations discussed above. Some of the EDS valuations for
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-3}
singularities were calculated in
Section

7.4
when analyzing the antisymmetric representation of
\mathfrak{su}(2k+1)
; the remaining ones are calculated in
Section

C.2
. Those for the
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-4}
,
\text{IV}^{*}
,
\text{III}^{*}
, and
\text{II}^{*}
singularity types are respectively calculated in
Section

C.1
,
Section

C.3
,
Section

C.4
, and
Section

C.5
.
Having found the EDS valuations for these different singularity types, we can show how to reproduce the
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
formulas for the
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
representations.

Vectors of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)

We considered two different seed models for the
\bm{(4k+2)}_{q}
representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, so we must determine the EDS valuations for these two models separately. We start with the
\nu=1
seed model. The generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,k,0,k+1)
along the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
. The EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-one behavior are given by
Eq.

161
with
n
replaced with
2k+1
and
s
set to 1:

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{2k+1}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{2k+1}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}

(225)

At the codimension-two locus supporting the seed model’s
\bm{(4k+2)}_{\frac{1}{2}}
matter, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,k+1,0,k+1)
. The EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-two behavior are therefore given by
Eq.

352
with
n
replaced by
2k+2
:

v(W_{m}^{(2)})=\frac{2k+2}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{2k+2}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(226)

The difference between these two sequences of EDS valuations is

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4}m^{2}+\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{2k+1}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{2k+2}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)

(227)

Using the relations

q=\frac{m}{2}\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m}_{4}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}=\overline{m}_{2}\,,

(228)

we can rewrite this as

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\SO(4k+2)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(4k+4)}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

(229)

The right-hand side of this expression exactly matches the expected formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
from the proposals, and the formula agrees with the observed orders of vanishing.

For the
\nu=0
seed model, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(0,0,0,0)
at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(4k+2)
factor. Because all the generating section components vanish to order 0, the EDS valuations
v(W_{m}^{(1)})
are
0
. At the codimension-two locus supporting the
\bm{(4k+2)}_{1}
matter, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,2,0,2)
, and the corresponding EDS valuations
v(W_{m}^{(2)})
are given by
Eq.

357
. We therefore have

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=v(W_{m}^{(2)})=\frac{m^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
1&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(230)

Using the relations

q=m\,,\quad\nu=0\,,\quad\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}=2\overline{m}_{2}\,,

(231)

this can be rewritten as

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\SO(4k+2)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(4k+4)}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

(232)

Again, the right-hand side agrees with expression for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
expected from the proposals and reproduces the observed orders of vanishing.

Vectors of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)

We considered two different seed models for the
\bm{4k}_{q}
representation of
\mathfrak{so}(4k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, so we need to perform separate EDS analyses for each of these models. We start with the
\nu=1
seed model. Along the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
gauge factor, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,k,0,k)
. Therefore, the EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-one behavior are given by
Eq.

352
with
n
set to
2k
:

v(W^{(1)}_{m})=\frac{2k}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{2k}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(233)

At the codimension-two locus supporting the
\bm{4k}_{1/2}
matter, the
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,k,0,k+1)
, and the EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-two behavior are given by
Eq.

161
with
n
set to
2k+1
and
s
set to
1
:

v(W^{(2)}_{m})=\frac{2k+1}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{2k+1}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}\,.

(234)

Using the relations
40
40

40

We are free to use either
\nu=\overline{m}_{2},\mu=\overline{m}_{4}
or
\nu=3\overline{m}_{2},\mu=\overline{3m}_{4}
, as the two options are related by the automorphism. One obtains similar results with either choice.

q=\frac{m}{2}\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m}_{2}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}=\overline{m}_{4}\,,

(235)

one can write the difference of the two EDS valuations as

v(W^{(2)}_{m})-v(W^{(1)}_{m})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\SO(4k)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(4k+2)}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

(236)

The right-hand side agrees with the expected formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
from the proposals and reproduces the observed order of vanishing data.

For the
\nu=2
seed model, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,2,0,2)
along the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(4k)
gauge algebra. The EDS valuations for the codimension-one behavior are therefore given by
Eq.

357
:

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{m^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
1&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(237)

At the codimension-two locus supporting the
\bm{4k}_{1}
matter,
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(2,3,1,4)
. The corresponding EDS valuations for this situations are given by
Eq.

363
:

v(W_{m}^{(2)})=m^{2}\,.

(238)

If we use the relations

q=m\,,\quad\nu=2\overline{m}_{2}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}=0\,,

(239)

the difference between these two EDS valuations can be written as

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+T_{\SO(4k)}(\nu)-T_{\SO(4k)}(\mu)}}\right)\,,

(240)

in agreement with the proposals.

Spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(8)

While we considered only one seed model, given by
Eq.

191
, for the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(8)
, we generated data using two different matter loci with distinct charges. We need to perform the EDS analysis separately for each of these loci. The codimension-one EDS valuations are the same for both these loci and are given by
Eq.

357
. The codimension-two EDS valuations, however, are different for the two cases.

At the loci
\{\sigma=c_{2,1}\pm b_{0,0}b_{2,1}=0\}
, which support matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
\frac{1}{2}
, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,2,0,3)
. Therefore, the codimension-two orders of vanishing are described by
Eq.

161
with
n=5
and
s=1
, and we have

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{4}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{5}{4}&m\nmid 2\end{cases}+\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
1&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(241)

If we note that
q=m/2
and use the appropriate expressions for
\nu
and
\mu
, this formula reproduces the
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
given in
Tables

16(b)
and
16(c)
and agrees with the proposed formulas in
Section

2.2
.

At the locus
\{\sigma=b_{0,0}=0\}
, which supports matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
1
, the generating section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(2,3,1,4)
. The EDS valuations for this situation, calculated in
Section

C.2
, are given by
v(W_{m}^{(2)})=m^{2}
. Therefore

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m^{2}+\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
1&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(242)

Noting that
q=m
and that
\mu=0
for this seed model, this expression again agrees with the proposals and the observed
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
values.

Spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(10)

For the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(10)
, we considered a single seed model given by
Eq.

176
. The section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,2,0,3)
at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\text{I}_{1}^{*s}
singularities and to orders
(2,2,0,3)
at the codimension-two locus supporting the
\bm{16}_{-\frac{1}{4}}
matter. To show the connection to EDS valuations, we should subtract the EDS valuations for
\text{I}^{*}_{1}
singularities in
Eq.

161
(with
n=5
,
s=1
) from those for
\text{IV}^{*}
singularities in
Eq.

369
:

\displaystyle v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{3}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&3\mid m\\
\frac{2}{3}&3\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)

(243)

\displaystyle-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{5}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{5}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{12}m^{2}+\begin{cases}0&\overline{m}_{4}=0\\
1&\overline{m}_{4}=2\\
\frac{5}{4}&\overline{m}_{4}=1,3\end{cases}-\begin{cases}0&\overline{m}_{3}=0\\
\frac{4}{3}&\overline{m}_{3}=1,2\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

Then, if we let

m=-4q\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m}_{4}\,,\quad\mu=\overline{(-m)}_{3}=\overline{4q}_{3}\,,

(244)

we find that

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{3}q^{2}+\begin{cases}0&\nu=0\\
1&\nu=2\\
\frac{5}{4}&\nu=1,3\end{cases}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
\frac{4}{3}&\mu=1,2\end{cases}}}\right)\,,

(245)

exactly in agreement with the proposed formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
.

Spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(12)

For the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(12)
, we considered only one seed model given by
Eq.

201
. At the codimension-one locus supporting the
\text{I}_{2}^{*}
singularities, the section components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to orders
(1,3,0,3)
. Therefore, the EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-one behavior are given by
Eq.

352
with
n=6
:

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{6}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{6}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(246)

At the codimension-two
\text{III}^{*}
locus supporting the
\bm{32^{\prime}}_{\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\bm{1}_{1}
matter, the section components vanish to orders
(2,3,1,4)
. The EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-two behavior, which are calculated in
Section

C.4.1
, are given by
v(W_{m}^{(2)})=m^{2}
. The difference of the EDS valuations is given by

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2}m^{2}+\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{3}{2}&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)\,.

(247)

After noting that
q=\frac{m}{2}
,
\nu=\overline{m}_{2}
or
3\overline{m}_{2}
, and
\mu=0
, we can rewrite this expression as

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2q^{2}+T_{\SO(12)}(\nu)-T_{\gE_{7}}(\mu)}}\right)\,.

(248)

Thus, we have recovered the expected expression for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
from
Section

2.2
.

Spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(14)

For the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(14)
, we considered a single seed model described by
Eq.

183
. The
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
sections components vanish to orders
(1,3,0,4)
at the codimension-one
\mathfrak{so}(14)
locus and to orders
(2,3,1,4)
at the codimension-two
\text{II}^{*}
matter locus. We therefore subtract the EDS valuation for
\text{I}^{*}_{3}
singularities given in
Eq.

161
(with
n=7
and
s=1
) from those for
\text{II}^{*}
singularities in
Eq.

391
:

\displaystyle v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=m^{2}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{7}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&4\mid m\\
1&(2\mid m)\text{ and }(4\nmid m)\\
\frac{7}{4}&(2\nmid m)\end{cases}}}\right)

(249)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}m^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&\overline{m}_{4}=0\\
1&\overline{m}_{4}=2\\
\frac{7}{4}&\overline{m}_{4}=1,3\end{cases}\,.

Letting

m=4q\,,\quad\nu=\overline{m}_{4}\,,\quad\mu=0\,,

(250)

we find that

v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4q^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&\nu=0\\
1&\nu=2\\
\frac{7}{4}&\nu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)\,,

(251)

exactly in agreement with the formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
expected from the proposals.

9
Representations of
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

For models with
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebras, we are primarily interested in matter in the
\bm{27}
representation of
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
. Such matter occurs at the intersection of the codimension-one
\text{IV}^{*}
locus supporting the
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
gauge algebra with the residual
\text{I}_{1}
discriminant locus. The singularity type enhances to
\text{III}^{*}
at this codimension-two locus, or
\gE_{7}
in the terms of ADE groups. The center of
\gE_{6}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, whose elements we denote by
\nu=0,1,2
. For a gauge group with a quotient involving the
\nu
element of the center, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for
\bm{27}
matter are then

\frac{\nu}{3}+j\text{ for }j\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(252)

In other words, a model with an
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra can support
\bm{27}_{q}
matter when the gauge group quotient involves the element

\nu=\overline{3q}_{3}\,.

(253)

of the
\gE_{6}
center. The allowed charges for
\nu=1
and
\nu=2
are equivalent up to a sign, as discussed in
Section

4.3
.

To find the orders of vanishing for the
\bm{27}
charges, we can start with the Weierstrass model

y^{2}=x^{3}+\sigma^{3}f_{3}xz^{4}+\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{2}^{2}-f_{3}\sigma x_{2}-x_{2}^{3}\sigma^{2}}}\right)z^{6}\,,

(254)

which admits the section
\hat{s}
with coordinates

[\hat{x}:\hat{y}:\hat{z}]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma^{2}x_{2}:\sigma^{2}y_{2}:1}}\right]\,,\quad\hat{w}=\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(f_{3}+3x_{2}^{2}\sigma}}\right)\,.

(255)

The
f
and
g
of the Weierstrass model are respectively proportional to
\sigma^{3}
and
\sigma^{4}
, and the order
\sigma^{4}
term in
g
is a perfect square. We therefore have an
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
gauge factor tuned along
\{\sigma=0\}
. Including the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
generated by the section above, the gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. This model is in fact equivalent to the
\mathcal{Q}(3,2,2,1,0,1,2)
model of
KuntzlerTateTrees
with
b_{0,0}
set to 1. By comparison to the spectrum given there, we can determine that the model supports

\bm{27}_{1/3}
matter at
\{\sigma=y_{2}=0\}
and that
\nu=1
. (Alternatively, we can take
\nu
to be 2 and say that
\{\sigma=y_{2}=0\}
supports
\bm{27}_{-1/3}
matter.)
There is also
\bm{1}_{1}
matter supported at
\{y_{2}=f_{3}+3x_{2}^{2}\sigma=0\}
. For a 6D F-theory model constructed from this elliptic fibration, this matter spectrum, together with the
1+\frac{1}{2}[\sigma]\cdot(K_{B}+[\sigma])
hypermultiplets of
\bm{78}_{0}
matter, satisfies the anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}-\frac{4}{3}[\sigma]
. The gauge group is therefore
(\gE_{6}\times\U(1))/\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.

The sections
m\hat{s}
realize pseudo-charge
m/3
at the
\bm{27}
locus
\{\sigma=y_{2}=0\}
with
\nu=\overline{m}_{3}
, giving us information about the orders of vanishing at
\bm{27}_{q}
loci for all of the allowed charges.
Table

20
lists the orders of vanishing at
\{\sigma=0\}
at
\{\sigma=y_{2}=0\}
for some of these pseudo-charges. The codimension-one orders of vanishing for a pseudo-charge
q
, which would correspond to a quotient involving the
\nu=\overline{3q}_{3}
element of the center, are described by the formula

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{6}}(\nu)\,.

(256)

And if we let

\mu=\overline{3q}_{2}\,,

(257)

the codimension-two orders of vanishing follow the expressions

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{2}q^{2}+T_{\gE_{6}}(\nu)-T_{\gE_{7}}(\mu)}}\right)

(258)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=(2,3,4)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{7}}(\mu)\,.

(259)

Since
d_{\gE_{6}}=3
and
d_{\gE_{7}}=2
, these formulas are in exact agreement with the proposals from
Section

2.2
. We expect that the generating section components should vanish to these same orders at genuine
\bm{27}_{q}
loci in
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models.

9.1
Connection to EDS valuations

Because all of the allowed combinations of
\nu
and pseudo-charge can be obtained from multiples of the generating section
\hat{s}
, we expect that
Eq.

258
should agree with formulas in
StangeEllTrouble
describing the valuations of elliptic divisibility sequences. As with our previous examples, we must consider two sets of EDS valuations:
v(W_{m}^{(1)})
for the codimension-one singularity type and
v(W_{m}^{(2)})
for the codimension-two singularity type. The EDS valuations for the codimension-one
\text{IV}^{*}
singularity type are calculated in
Section

C.3
and are given by
Eq.

369
:

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{2}{3}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&3\mid m\\
\frac{2}{3}&3\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(260)

The EDS valuations for the codimension-two
\text{III}^{*}
singularities, meanwhile, are calculated in
Section

C.4.2
, and are given by
Eq.

383
:

v(W_{m}^{(2)})=\frac{3}{4}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{3}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(261)

The orders of vanishing of
\hat{z}
at codimension-two for multiples
m\hat{s}
of the generating section
\hat{s}
should then be given by
v(W_{m}^{(2)})-v(W_{m}^{(1)})
:

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{3}{4}m^{2}-\frac{2}{3}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{3}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}+\begin{cases}0&3\mid m\\
\frac{2}{3}&3\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(262)

Since
m\hat{s}
admits pseudo-charge
q=\frac{m}{3}
for the
\bm{27}
matter, this expression can be rewritten as

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{3}{4}q^{2}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid(3q)\\
\frac{3}{4}&2\nmid(3q)\end{cases}+\begin{cases}0&3\mid(3q)\\
\frac{2}{3}&3\nmid(3q)\end{cases}\,.

(263)

Recalling that
\nu=\overline{3q}_{3}
and that
\mu=\overline{3q}_{2}
, this formula exactly matches
Eq.

258
, as expected.

10
Representations of
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

For models with
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebras, we are mostly interested in matter in the
\bm{56}
representation. The center of
\gE_{7}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, whose elements we denote as
\nu=0,1
. As discussed in
Section

4.3
, when the quotient involves the
\nu
element of the center, the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for
\bm{56}
matter are

\frac{\nu}{2}+j\text{ for }j\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(264)

In other words, a model with an
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra can support
\bm{56}_{q}
matter when the quotient involves the element of the center corresponding to

\nu=\overline{2q}_{2}\,.

(265)

In F-theory models,
\bm{56}
matter occurs at codimension-two loci where the singularity type enhances from from
\text{III}^{*}
to
\text{II}^{*}
. Phrased in the ADE language, the singularity type enhances from
\gE_{7}
to
\gE_{8}
. The
\bm{56}
representation is pseudoreal, and if the supported
\bm{56}
matter is uncharged under the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
, it can be realized as half-hypermultiplets in 6D models. The fibers at such loci would take the shape of an incompletely resolved
\gE_{8}
diagram
MorrisonTaylorMaS
;
KanHalfHyper
. However, if the
\bm{56}
matter has a nonzero
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge, the total representation, which includes the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge, is no longer pseudoreal. Therefore,
\bm{56}_{q}
matter can only come in full hypermultiplets in 6D when
q
is nonzero, and the fibers at the corresponding codimension-two loci form completely resolved
\gE_{8}
diagrams. Since we are almost exclusively interested in
\mathfrak{u}(1)
-charged
\bm{56}
matter, we primarily deal with full hypermultiplets. The pseudoreal nature of the
\bm{56}
representation also implies that, in line with the arguments in
Section

5.1
, the sign of the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge is unimportant for
\bm{56}
matter.

The codimension-two
\text{II}^{*}
loci admitting complete resolutions support both
\bm{56}
and singlet matter. Specifically, in 6D models, an irreducible codimension-two locus with a completely resolvable
\text{II}^{*}
singular fiber supports a full hypermultiplet of
\bm{56}
matter and a full hypermultiplet of singlet matter. Just as was the case for the spinors of
\mathfrak{so}(12)
, the appearance of this extra singlet can be understood using the Katz–Vafa method and the following branching rule:

\displaystyle\gE_{8}

\displaystyle\to\gE_{7}\times\SU(2)\,,

(266)

\displaystyle\bm{248}

\displaystyle\to(\bm{133},\bm{1})\oplus(\bm{56},\bm{2})\oplus(\bm{1},\bm{3})\,.

The
(\bm{56},\bm{2})
degrees of freedom combine to form the full hypermultiplet of
\bm{56}
matter. The singlet hypermultiplet is formed by the degrees of freedom corresponding to the positive and negative roots of the
(\bm{1},\bm{3})
in this decomposition.

This additional singlet can also have a
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge, raising the question of how this singlet’s charge compares to the charge of the
\bm{56}
matter. In the resolved
\gE_{8}
fiber at the codimension-two locus, there are two curves,
\gamma_{+}
and
\gamma_{-}
, which represent the highest
\bm{56}
weights in the
(\bm{56},\bm{2})
of the decomposition. The
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for these
\bm{56}
weights should be negatives of each other, implying that

q_{\bm{56}}=\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{+}=-\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{-}\,,

(267)

where
\hat{s}
is the generating section for the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
. These two weights are also the highest and lowest weights of an
\mathfrak{su}(2)
fundamental, so the curves are related by

\gamma_{+}=\gamma_{-}+c\,.

(268)

The curve
c
represents a root of the
(\bm{1},\bm{3})
part of the decomposition, and it corresponds to the singlet matter. The charge of this singlet is

q_{\bm{1}}=\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot c=\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{+}-\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{-}=2\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot\gamma_{+}=2q_{56}\,.

(269)

In other words, the charge of the singlet supported at the
\text{II}^{*}
locus should be twice that of
\bm{56}
matter. Since the singlet representation is real, the arguments in
Section

5.1
suggest that the sign of the singlet charge does not matter.

We can now investigate how the orders of vanishing of the section components encode the charges of the
\bm{56}
matter and the associated singlet. We start with the Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{1}{3}\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3b^{2}c_{0,3}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c_{2,2}^{2}-3c_{1,3}c_{3,1}}}\right)}}\right)xz^{4}

(270)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{108}\sigma^{5}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(9b^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3c_{1,3}^{2}\sigma-8c_{0,3}c_{2,2}}}\right)+4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(27c_{0,3}c_{3,1}^{2}-9c_{1,3}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}\sigma+2c_{2,2}^{3}\sigma}}\right)}}\right)z^{6}\,,

which admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with components

z\begin{gathered}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[c_{3,1}^{2}\sigma^{2}-\frac{2}{3}b^{2}c_{2,2}\sigma^{2}:-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b^{4}c_{1,3}-2b^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}+2c_{3,1}^{3}}}\right):b}}\right]\,,\\
\hat{w}=-\sigma^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b^{6}c_{0,3}-b^{4}c_{2,2}^{2}\sigma-b^{4}c_{1,3}c_{3,1}\sigma+4b^{2}c_{2,2}c_{3,1}^{2}\sigma-3c_{3,1}^{4}\sigma}}\right)\,.\end{gathered}

(271)

This model is equivalent to the
\mathcal{Q}(3,3,2,1,0,1,2)
model of
KuntzlerTateTrees
after trivial redefinitions of the parameters. It supports an
\mathfrak{e}_{7}
gauge algebra along
\{\sigma=0\}
. In 6D F-theory models constructed from this elliptic fibration, the codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=b=0\}
supports full hypermultiplets of
\bm{56}_{1/2}\oplus\bm{1}_{1}
matter, in line with the observations in
KuntzlerTateTrees
.
The model also supports
\bm{1}_{2}
hypermultiplets at
\{b=c_{3,1}=0\}
and
\bm{1}_{1}
hypermultiplets at

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\hat{y}}{\sigma^{3}}=\frac{\hat{w}}{\sigma^{3}}=0}}\right\}\textbackslash\{b=c_{3,1}=0\}\,.

(272)

While
f
and
g
vanish to orders
4
and
6
at the codimension-two locus
\{\sigma=c_{0,3}=0\}
, one can avoid the problems created by this locus by choosing the classes of the parameters such that
[\sigma]\cdot[c_{0,3}]=0
. For instance, one can choose the divisor classes such that
[c_{0,3}]=0
, in which case
[b]=-2K_{B}{}-\frac{3}{2}[\sigma]
. In this situation, one can verify that the charged matter spectrum satisfies the gauge and mixed gauge–gravitational anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}{}+2[b]-\frac{3}{2}[\sigma]
. From the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the
\bm{56}
matter, one can see that the generating section, and therefore the model as a whole, has
\nu=1
.

We now consider multiples of the generating section
\hat{s}
. The
\bm{56}
matter at
\{\sigma=b=0\}
would have pseudo-charge
q=\frac{1}{2}m
for the section
m\hat{s}
with
\nu=\overline{m}_{2}
. The
m\hat{s}
sections therefore give us analogues for all of the possible
\bm{56}
charges described
Eq.

264
.
Table

21
lists the orders of vanishing of the
m\hat{s}
section components for various values of
m
, which follow a clear pattern. The codimension-one orders of vanishing are described by the equations

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{7}}(\nu)\,.

(273)

The codimension-two orders of vanishing are given by

\displaystyle\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2q^{2}+T_{\gE_{7}}(\nu)}}\right)\,,

(274)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})\,.

(275)

The center of
\gE_{8}
is trivial, and
\mu
,
T_{\gE_{8}}(\mu)
, and
\vec{\tau}_{\gE_{8}}(\mu)
are all trivial as well. These formulas therefore agree with the expectations from
Section

2.2
.

; pseudo-charge

. One should observe the same orders of vanishing in Weierstrass models supporting
\bm{56}_{q}\oplus\bm{1}_{2q}
matter.

10.1
Connection to EDS valuations

Since all of the allowed combinations of
\nu
and pseudo-charge
q
can be obtained from multiples of the generating section
\hat{s}
, we expect that the formulas for the orders of vanishing above should agree with the formulas for EDS valuations in
StangeEllTrouble
. As before, we start by finding the EDS valuations corresponding to the codimension-one behavior and the codimension-two behavior of
Eq.

270
. For the
\text{III}^{*}
singularities at codimension-one, the generating section
271
vanishes to orders

(2,2,0,3)
at
\{\sigma=0\}
. The corresponding EDS valuations are calculated by a procedure similar to that in
Section

C.4.2
and are given by
Eq.

383
:

v(W_{m}^{(1)})=\frac{3}{4}m^{2}-\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{3}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(276)

For the
\text{II}^{*}
singularities at codimension-two, the generating section
271
vanishes to orders
(2,3,1,4)
at
\{\sigma=b=0\}
. The codimension-two EDS valuations are calculated by a procedure similar to that in
Section

C.5
and are given by
Eq.

391
:

v(W_{m}^{(2)})=m^{2}\,.

(277)

The formula for
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
of the
m\hat{s}
sections should be given by the difference

v(W^{(2)}_{m})-v(W^{(1)}_{m})=\frac{1}{4}m^{2}+\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{3}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

(278)

Noting that the section
m\hat{s}
realizes pseudo-charge
q=\frac{m}{2}
for the
\bm{56}
matter, we should expect that

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=q^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\overline{2q}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2q^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\overline{2q}_{2}}}\right)\,,

(279)

exactly in agreement with the observed order of vanishing data,
Eq.

274
, and the proposals in
Section

2.2
.

11
Matter charged under multiple gauge algebra factors

Up to this point, we have only analyzed situations where the gauge algebra is either
\mathfrak{u}(1)
or
\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a simple Lie algebra. However, F-theory models can have more complicated gauge algebras consisting of multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
and nonabelian factors. Matter in such models can be charged under many of these factors. For instance, an F-theory model might have an
\mathfrak{su}(m)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra and matter in the
(\bm{m},\overline{\bm{n}})_{q_{1},q_{2}}
representation. This section focuses on these situations. We divide the analysis into two parts. We first discuss how to approach situations with matter charged under multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors. We then turn to matter charged under multiple simple nonabelian factors and (at least) one
\mathfrak{u}(1)
. Despite the added complications in these examples, we will see that the connections between the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges and orders of vanishing proposed in
Section

2.2
still hold.

11.1
Multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors

If there are
L

\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors, the Mordell–Weil rank of the corresponding elliptic fibration should be
L
, and each
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factor corresponds to a particular generating section. The formulas in
Section

2.2
should hold without modification for each generating section. More specifically, if the nonabelian part of the gauge algebra is simple and nontrivial, each generating section has an associated integer
\nu
that specifies which component of the resolved fiber at codimension-one is hit by the section.
41
41

41

If the nonabelian part of the gauge algebra is semisimple with
K
simple nonabelian factors, there are a total of
K\times L
integers playing the role of
\nu
, one for each combination of generating section and simple nonabelian factor.
Along the codimension-one locus supporting this nonabelian gauge algebra, the section components for a particular generating section should vanish to the orders dictated by its
\nu
integer. If some matter has a certain charge under a particular
\mathfrak{u}(1)
, the section components for the corresponding generating section should vanish to the orders proposed in
Section

2.2
at the matter locus. To determine some matter field’s charge under all of the
L

\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors, one would apply the procedures outlined in
Section

2.2
to each generating section.

Note that there can be different choices for the basis of these
\mathfrak{u}(1)
’s when there are multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors. The
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges of matter can change if one uses a different
\mathfrak{u}(1)
basis. In the elliptic fibration, the basis of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras corresponds to the basis of generating sections. If one changes the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
basis, the generating section basis should change as well, and the new
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges should agree with the orders of vanishing for the new basis sections as per the rules in
Section

2.2
. Thus, even though the charge spectrum changes with the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
basis, the corresponding change in the basis of sections should ensure that the formulas in
Section

2.2
still hold. As long as one maintains a consistent relation between the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
basis and the basis of generating sections, changing the basis of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras should not pose any problems.

Given that the formulas in
Section

2.2
are essentially unchanged when there are multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors, we will not extensively discuss examples of this type here. However,
Section

12
lists some models in the prior F-theory literature with multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors, all of which agree with the ideas discussed here and in
Section

2.2
.

11.2
Multiple nonabelian factors

Now let us consider the case where the nonabelian part of the gauge algebra has multiple simple factors. Specifically, suppose the gauge algebra is

\bigoplus_{\kappa=1}^{K}\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\,,

(280)

where each
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
is a simple Lie algebra. As before, we assume that the
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
are all simply-laced. Because the argument above suggests that the presence of multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors does not significantly change the analysis, we assume for simplicity that there is only a single
\mathfrak{u}(1)
associated with a generating section
\hat{s}
. Each
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
is supported along a codimension-one locus
\{\sigma_{\kappa}=0\}
in the base with singular fibers of the appropriate type. We also have integers
\nu_{\kappa}
describing which resolved fiber component along
\{\sigma_{\kappa}=0\}
is hit by
\hat{s}
. According to
Section

2.2
, the section components for
\hat{s}
should vanish to the orders described by
Eq.

17
at each of the
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
loci:

\ord_{\sigma_{k}=0}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ord_{\sigma_{k}=0}(\hat{x}),\ord_{\sigma_{k}=0}(\hat{y}),\ord_{\sigma_{k}=0}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{G_{\kappa}}(\nu_{\kappa})\,.

(281)

Here,
G_{\kappa}
is the universal covering group for
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
. Now consider matter that is charged under multiple
\mathfrak{g}_{\kappa}
and that is supported at the codimension-two intersection locus of the corresponding
\{\sigma_{\kappa}=0\}
. If the singularity type enhances at this matter locus to one associated with the group
H
, the section components should vanish to the orders specified by Equations
18
and
19
for some
\mu
in the center of
H
. The index
i
in
Eq.

18
runs over those simple nonabelian algebras under which the matter is charged.

To illustrate these ideas, let us focus on models with
\mathfrak{su}(m)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebras and matter in the bifundamental
(\bm{m},\overline{\bm{n}})_{q}
representation. At the codimension-two locus supporting the bifundamental matter, the singularity type enhances from
\text{I}_{m}^{s}\times\text{I}_{n}^{s}
(or
\SU(m)\times\SU(n)
) to
\text{I}_{m+n}^{s}
(or
\SU(m+n)
). If we have integers
(\nu_{m},\nu_{n})
describing the fiber components hit by
\hat{s}
, then the allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for this bifundamental matter are given by

\frac{\nu_{m}}{m}-\frac{\nu_{n}}{n}+j\text{ for }j\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(282)

According to the formulas in
Section

2.2
, the section components of
\hat{s}
should vanish to the orders

\begin{gathered}\ord_{\sigma_{m}=0}(\hat{z})=0\,,\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ord_{\sigma_{m}=0}(\hat{x}),\ord_{\sigma_{m}=0}(\hat{y}),\ord_{\sigma_{m}=0}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\SU(m)}(\nu_{m})=(0,1,1)\times u_{m}(\nu_{m})\end{gathered}

(283)

at the codimension-one locus
\{\sigma_{m}=0\}
supporting the
\mathfrak{su}(m)
algebra and to the orders

\begin{gathered}\ord_{\sigma_{n}=0}(\hat{z})=0\,,\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ord_{\sigma_{n}=0}(\hat{x}),\ord_{\sigma_{n}=0}(\hat{y}),\ord_{\sigma_{n}=0}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\vec{\tau}_{\SU(n)}(\nu_{n})=(0,1,1)\times u_{n}(\nu_{n})\end{gathered}

(284)

at the codimension-one locus
\{\sigma_{n}=0\}
supporting the
\mathfrak{su}(n)
algebra. At the matter locus, the
\hat{z}
component of the generating section should vanish to order

\displaystyle\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{mn}{m+n}q^{2}+T_{\SU(m)}(\nu_{m})+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu_{n})-T_{\SU(m+n)}(\mu)}}\right)

(285)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{mn}{m+n}q^{2}+\frac{\nu_{m}(m-\nu_{m})}{m}+\frac{\nu_{n}(n-\nu_{n})}{n}-\frac{\mu(m+n-\mu)}{m+n}}}\right)\,,

where
\mu
is an integer representing an element of the center of
\SU(m+n)
. Additionally, we would expect that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=(2,3,4)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\vec{\tau}_{\SU(m+n)}(\mu)

(286)

One can verify that this formula gives integral
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
if

\mu=\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(mq+\nu_{n}+\frac{m}{n}\nu_{n}}}\right)}_{m+n}\,.

(287)

This expression for
\mu
also reduces to the
\mathfrak{su}(m)
fundamental
\mu
expression when
n=1
.

We can check these proposals for the bifundamentals by looking at a model with an
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra. This example is given by the Weierstrass model

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{3}+c_{1}c_{3}-c_{0}b^{2}}}\right)xz^{4}

(288)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{27}c_{2}^{3}-\frac{2}{3}b^{2}c_{0}c_{2}+\frac{1}{4}b^{2}c_{1}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}c_{1}c_{3}c_{2}+c_{0}c_{3}^{2}}}\right)z^{6}\,,

where

\begin{gathered}c_{0}=\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({b_{2}^{\prime}}^{2}+4{c_{0}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}}}\right)\,,\\
c_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}^{2}({b_{1}^{\prime}}{b_{2}^{\prime}}+2{c_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4})\,,\\
c_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(4\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}c_{2}^{\prime}+{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)\,,\\
c_{3}=\frac{b{b_{1}^{\prime}}}{2}+c_{3}^{\prime}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}\,.\end{gathered}

(289)

Because the discriminant is proportional to
\sigma_{4}^{4}\sigma_{5}^{5}
and the split conditions are satisfied, this model supports an
\mathfrak{su}(4)
algebra on
\{\sigma_{4}=0\}
and an
\mathfrak{su}(5)
algebra on
\{\sigma_{5}=0\}
. Additionally, it admits a generating section
\hat{s}
with section components

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{b{b_{1}^{\prime}}}{2}+c_{3}^{\prime}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{6}b^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}+4c_{2}^{\prime}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}}}\right)\,,

(290)

\displaystyle\hat{y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}\Big(b^{4}{b_{1}^{\prime}}{b_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{5}+2b^{4}{c_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}-2b^{3}{b_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{2}^{\prime}}+2b^{2}{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}{c_{3}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-4b^{2}{c_{2}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}+6b{b_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{2}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}+4{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{3}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{2}\Big)\,,

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=b\,,

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}\Big(b^{6}{b_{2}^{\prime}}^{2}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}^{3}+4b^{6}{c_{0}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{3}-b^{5}{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}{b_{2}^{\prime}}\sigma_{5}-2b^{5}{b_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}+2b^{4}{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}{c_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle-2b^{4}{b_{1}^{\prime}}{b_{2}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}^{2}-4b^{4}{c_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{2}-4b^{4}{c_{2}^{\prime}}^{2}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}

\displaystyle-2b^{3}{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{3}{c_{3}^{\prime}}+16b^{3}{b_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{2}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}-14b^{2}{b_{1}^{\prime}}^{2}{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{2}\sigma_{4}\sigma_{5}

\displaystyle+16b^{2}{c_{2}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{2}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{2}-24b{b_{1}^{\prime}}{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{3}\sigma_{4}^{2}\sigma_{5}^{2}-12{c_{3}^{\prime}}^{4}\sigma_{4}^{3}\sigma_{5}^{3}\Big)\,.

The complete gauge algebra is therefore
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
. The charged matter spectrum, which is summarized in
Table

22
, satisfies the anomaly cancellation conditions with
0pt=-2K_{B}+2[b]-\frac{3}{4}[\sigma_{4}]-\frac{4}{5}[\sigma_{5}]
. As can be seen from the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges, we can take
\nu_{4}
and
\nu_{5}
to both be 1;
this agrees with the orders of vanishing of
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
at
\{\sigma_{4}=0\}
and
\{\sigma_{5}=0\}
. The codimension-two orders of vanishing at the matter loci in
Table

also agree with the formulas discussed previously.

through

. Thus, the proposals in

hold for the

.

:
Orders of vanishing for
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
at codimension one and two for combinations of
\nu_{4}
,

, and pseudo-charge

for
(\bm{4},\overline{\bm{5}})
bifundamental matter. These data are found using the
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
construction described by Equations
288
and
289
.

These same ideas should be applicable for representations beyond the
(\bm{m},\overline{\bm{n}})
bifundamentals. One can also have
(\bm{m},\bm{n})_{q}
bifundamentals of
\mathfrak{su}(m)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, whose allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges are given by

\frac{\nu_{m}}{m}+\frac{\nu_{n}}{n}+j\text{ for }j\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(292)

These bifundamentals also occur at the intersection of codimension-one
\text{I}_{m}^{*s}
and
\text{I}_{n}^{*s}
loci where the singularity type enhances to
\text{I}_{m+n}^{*s}
. Therefore, we expect that, for instance,

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{mn}{m+n}q^{2}+T_{\SU(m)}(\nu_{m})+T_{\SU(n)}(\nu_{n})-T_{\SU(m+n)}(\mu)}}\right)

(293)

for some
\mu
representing an element of
\mathbb{Z}_{m+n}
, the center of
\SU(m+n)
. This formula gives integer
\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
for

\mu=\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(mq-\nu_{n}-\frac{m}{n}\nu_{n}}}\right)}_{m+n}\,,

(294)

and the expression expression for
\mu
also reduces to that for
\mathfrak{su}(m)
fundamentals when
n=1
. The proposals in
Section

2.2
should also cover other types of matter charged under multiple nonabelian gauge algebras, such as
(\bm{m},\bm{n})_{q}
matter charged under
\mathfrak{so}(m)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras. However, there are also matter representations involving only a simple nonabelian algebra that occur at singularities of a gauge divisor. If the gauge algebra is
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, for instance, one can have matter in the adjoint or symmetric representations of
\mathfrak{su}(n)
localized at double point singularities of the gauge divisor
SadovGreenSchwarz
;
MorrisonTaylorMaS
;
CveticKleversPiraguaTaylor
;
AndersonGrayRaghuramTaylorMiT
;
KleversTaylor
;
KleversEtAlExotic
. If we focus on a sufficiently small neighborhood near the matter locus, these situations more closely resemble the bifundamental matter situations considered above: the matter locus locally appears to be the intersection of two codimension-one
\text{I}_{n}^{*s}
loci, even though these two components are actually connected due to global properties of the gauge divisor. If such matter also has a
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge, the orders of vanishing of the section components may be described by formulas akin to those for bifundamental matter. These more exotic matter representations lie outside of the scope of this paper, so we do not rigorously analyze them here. Nevertheless, they pose an interesting direction for future work.

12
Examples in explicit constructions

We have so far performed somewhat indirect tests of the proposals in
Section

2.2
. Specifically, we have used multiples of generating sections admitting relatively small
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges as proxies for genuine generating sections admitting larger
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges. Underlying this strategy is the assumption that both types of sections should behave similarly at the appropriate loci. Since the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge only depends on the local behavior of sections near a matter locus, this assumption is reasonable. This strategy of course lets us circumvent the difficulties in finding F-theory models admitting large charges.

However, we can also test the proposals by examining previous F-theory models with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras. We have verified that, for the following models in the prior literature, the massless charged matter spectrum agrees with the formulas in
Section

2.2
:

•

The Morrison–Park construction in
MorrisonParkU1
, which has a
\U(1)
gauge group and admits
\bm{1}_{1}
and
\bm{1}_{2}
matter.

•

The
\U(1)
constructions in
KleversEtAlToric
and
Raghuram34
that admit
\bm{1}_{1}
,
\bm{1}_{2}
, and
\bm{1}_{3}
matter.

•

The
\U(1)
construction in
Raghuram34
that admits
\bm{1}_{1}
,
\bm{1}_{2}
,
\bm{1}_{3}
, and
\bm{1}_{4}
matter.

•

The
\U(1)\times\U(1)
models in
CveticKleversPiraguaMultU1
and
CveticKleversPiraguaTaylor
. By looking at linear combinations of the generating sections, we can also recover the relative signs of the charges under the two
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors.

•

The
\U(1)\times\U(1)\times\U(1)
model in
CveticKleversPiraguaSong
. Again, we can also recover the relative signs of charges under the three
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors by considering linear combinations of the generating sections.

•

The models in
KleversEtAlExotic
with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras, namely the
F_{2}
,
F_{3}
,
F_{5}
,
F_{6}
,
F_{7}
,
F_{8}
,
F_{9}
,
F_{12}
,
F_{14}
, and
F_{15}
models. Note that the charge spectra are derived using non-Weierstrass descriptions of the elliptic fibrations, so when there are multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebras, once must be careful in matching the basis of Weierstrass generating sections to the basis of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges.

•

The
\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
\mathfrak{so}(10)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, and
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models in
KuntzlerTateTrees
. While
KuntzlerTateTrees
includes models with other gauge algebras, matter spectra are not listed for these alternative models. Note that the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges are given in an alternative set of units where all charges, including those for matter also charged under a nonabelian algebra, are integral. The lattice spacing for singlet charges in this convention may be an integer larger than 1. While these models are tunings of elliptic fibrations with fibers embedded
\mathbb{P}^{1,1,2}
, we can form the equivalent Weierstrass models by performing the same tunings in the Morrison–Park Weierstrass model given in
MorrisonParkU1
.

•

The
\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
, and
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models in
BorchmannSU5Top
. Again, the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges in these models are given in an alternative set of units where all charges are integral. The
\mathfrak{su}(5)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
and
\mathfrak{su}(4)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models given there are either equivalent to or specializations of models in
KuntzlerTateTrees
.

•

The
\mathfrak{su}(3)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model in
RaghuramTaylorTurnerSM
, which is a generalization of the
\mathfrak{su}(3)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model in
KleversEtAlToric
. The
\mathfrak{su}(5)
enhancement discussion in
RaghuramTaylorTurnerSM
also makes use of a “would-be”
(\bm{3},\bm{2})_{-5/6}
matter locus. At this would-be locus, the section components vanish to the orders appropriate for
(\bm{3},\bm{2})_{-5/6}
matter according to the equations in
Sections

2.2
and
11.2
.

•

The models involving
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors in
RaghuramTaylorTurnerEnhancement
, including the
\mathfrak{su}(18)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
and
\mathfrak{su}(19)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
models. While these models were not analyzed using resolutions, the matter spectra given there, which can be recovered with the techniques presented here, agree with the anomaly cancellation conditions and the expectations from Higgsing related nonabelian models.

These models provide a broad set of gauge algebras, covering many of the representations discussed here. Many of them also have multiple
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factors and matter charged under multiple nonabelian factors, in further support of the ideas in
Section

11
. The fact that a variety of models in independent works follow the formulas in
Section

2.2
, together with the evidence presented in previous sections, suggests that our proposals should hold more broadly.

13
Conclusions

To summarize, we proposed a series of formulas, outlined in
Section

2.2
, that relate the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of matter supported at a particular locus to the orders of vanishing of the generating section’s components at that locus. As discussed in
Section

12
, these formulas accurately reproduce the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge spectrum of various F-theory models from the prior literature. They also correctly describe the results of the indirect method of
Section

5
for analyzing large
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges, and they appear to be related to formulas from
StangeEllTrouble
for the
p
-adic valuations of elliptic divisibility sequences. In addition to offering a way of quickly determining the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges of light matter supported in an F-theory model, the proposals can aid explorations of the F-theory landscape by predicting the geometric properties of models admitting desired charge spectra.

There are several natural directions to pursue in future work. First, it is important to determine how these proposals would apply in models with gauge algebras and nonabelian matter representations beyond those covered here. For instance, our analysis focused on matter charged under abelian and simply-laced nonabelian gauge factors, but one might expect similar formulas to hold when non-simply-laced gauge factors are present as well. One possibility is that the proposals are essentially unchanged even when the gauge algebra is non-simply laced. Even if matter is charged under a
\mathfrak{u}(1)
factor and a non-simply-laced nonabelian factor, one would take the Lie group
G
for the nonabelian factor to be the simply-laced Lie group associated with the codimension-one singularity type. The only practical difference would then be that monodromy effects would restrict the allowed values of
\nu
and
\mu
. This is just a tentative suggestion, however, and more work should be done to systematically investigate how the formulas may change for matter charged under non-simply-laced factors.

Furthermore, the proposals should be checked in models admitting
\mathfrak{u}(1)
-charged matter additionally charged in exotic nonabelian representations, such as the adjoint,
42
42

42

While the adjoint representation by itself is not typically viewed as an exotic representations, it is difficult to find F-theory constructions with adjoint matter that also has a
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge. Such situations would likely require adjoint matter localized at double point singularities
MorrisonTaylorMaS
;
KleversEtAlExotic
.
symmetric, and three-index antisymmetric representations of
\mathfrak{su}(n)
. Situations where the codimension-two singularity type enhances by a rank greater than one are also worthy of examination. An intriguing question is whether our proposals apply for
\mathfrak{u}(1)
models with superconformal matter loci, where
f
and
g
vanish to orders
(4,6)
or greater at codimension two. If so, our formulas could provide interesting insights into
\U(1)
flavor symmetries of 6D SCFTs
LeeRegaladoWeigand
;
ApruzzietalU1SCFT
.

A second important direction is clarifying the formulas’ exact range of validity. At the same time, we should better understand why the proposals work from both mathematical and physical perspectives. The seemingly related formulas in
StangeEllTrouble
describing EDS valuations may provide some insights into geometric explanations for the proposals. In fact, the parallels between these two contexts suggest an intriguing connection between topics in physics and number theory. This possibility makes understanding the physics behind the proposals all the more important. In particular, one might hope that a physical understanding of the proposals may translate to new perspectives on elliptic divisibility sequences and related mathematical topics. Alternatively, it may be possible to definitively establish the proposals by considering intersection numbers in the full elliptic fibration. The charge itself is given by
\sigma(\hat{s})\cdot c
, suggesting that the proposals could be understood in terms of similar intersection numbers. This approach would likely require a more concrete understanding of how the wrapping behavior of sections at codimension-two loci is encapsulated in the section components.

While the analysis here focused on models in Weierstrass form, there may be similar relations between
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges and orders of vanishing for elliptic fibrations in alternative forms, such as those in Tate form
BershadskyEtAlSingularities
;
KatzEtAlTate
or those described as hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{P}^{1,1,2}

MorrisonParkU1
;
KuntzlerTateTrees
. It is oftentimes more convenient to work with these alternative forms, and an easy method for reading off
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges in such situations could simplify several analyses.

Finally, it would be interesting to use the proposals to characterize the landscape and swampland of F-theory
\mathfrak{u}(1)
models. A variety of important open problems involve understanding which models with “large”
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges can occur in F-theory. Even for the simplest case of models with just a
\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra, determining a bound on the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges of singlet matter and constructing F-theory models with singlet charges larger than 6 (in appropriately scaled units) remain key goals in the F-theory program. Because they seem to work for arbitrarily large charges, the proposals would allow us to glean information about models with larger charges without directly constructing them. Thus, they may show the way towards constructions with new types of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges, such as singlet charges larger than
6
. Alternatively, the proposals may enable one to anticipate geometric obstructions to realizing particular
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges. Such observations could, for instance, lead to new bounds on the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges and charge spectra that can occur in F-theory. A better physical understanding of the proposals may also reveal other constraints on
\mathfrak{u}(1)
algebras in F-theory. In general, a clearer knowledge of the geometric properties of
\mathfrak{u}(1)
-charged matter can offer new insights into F-theory.

We would like to thank Washington Taylor for helpful discussions, and Timo Weigand and Antonella Grassi for useful comments. The work of APT was supported by DOE grant DE-SC00012567 and DOE (HEP) Award DE-SC0013528. The work of NR was supported by NSF grant PHY-1720321.

Appendix A
Expressions for specific gauge algebras and representations

A.1
Singlets

Charged singlets occur at codimension-two loci in the base of the elliptic fibration with
\text{I}_{2}
singular fibers. In units corresponding to the definition of Shioda map in
Eq.

9
, charged singlets should have integer charges. At a codimension-two locus supporting singlets with charge
q
, the
\hat{z}
component vanishes to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{q^{2}}{4}-\frac{\overline{q}_{2}}{4}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor\frac{q^{2}}{4}}}\right\rfloor\,,

(295)

while the
\hat{x}
,
\hat{y}
, and
\hat{w}
components vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,1,1}}\right)\overline{q}_{2}

(296)

Based on these equations, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at a codimension-two singlet locus, one can determine the charge
q
of the supported matter (up to sign) as

q^{2}=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y})+\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})\,.

(297)

A.2
\mathfrak{su}(n)

For models with an
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra, we label elements of the center of
\SU(n)
with the parameter
\nu
, which can run from 0 to
n-1
. The section components in a model with a quotient corresponding to
\nu
vanish to the following orders at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{su}(n)
algebra:

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,1,1}}\right)\times u_{n}(\nu)\,.

(298)

Fundamentals

An
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model can have fundamental matter with charge
q=\frac{\nu}{n}+j
for integers
j
. In other words, fundamental matter with charge
q
can occur when
\nu
is
\overline{nq}_{n}
. At the codimension-two locus supporting the
\bm{n}_{q}
matter, the
\hat{z}
component should vanish to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{n+1}q^{2}+\frac{\nu(n-\nu)}{n}-\frac{\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1-\mu}}\right)}{(n+1)}}}\right)\,,

(299)

where

\mu=\overline{nq}_{n+1}\,.

(300)

The other section components should vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,1,1}}\right)\times u_{n+1}(\mu)\,.

(301)

Based on these equations, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{su}(n)
factor and the codimension-two fundamental locus, one can determine the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q
of the supported fundamental matter (up to sign) as

q^{2}={\frac{2(n+1)}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\frac{\tilde{\mu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1-\tilde{\mu}}}\right)}{2(n+1)}-\frac{\tilde{\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-\tilde{\nu}}}\right)}{2n}}}\right)}\,,

(302)

where

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\nu}=\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y})=\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})\,,\\
\tilde{\mu}=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y})-3\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})\,.\end{gathered}

(303)

Antisymmetric matter for odd
n

An
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model with discrete quotient
\nu
can have antisymmetric matter with charge
q=\frac{2\nu}{n}+j
for integers
j
. For odd
n
(i.e.,
n=2k+1
), this means that antisymmetric matter with charge
q
can occur when

\nu=\overline{nq(k+1)}_{n}\,.

(304)

At the codimension-two locus supporting the antisymmetric matter, the
\hat{z}
component should vanish to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{4}q^{2}+\frac{\nu(n-\nu)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\mu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)

(305)

with

\mu=\overline{nq}_{4}\,.

(306)

The other section components vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)\\
=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\mu=0\\
(1,2,2)&\mu=2\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rfloor,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rceil}}\right)&\mu=1,3\end{cases}

(307)

If one knows the orders of vanishing at the codimension-one
\mathfrak{su}(n)
locus and at the codimension-two antisymmetric locus, the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the supported matter can be determined (up to sign) as

q^{2}=\frac{8}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})-\frac{\tilde{\nu}(n-\tilde{\nu})}{2n}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=0\\
1&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rceil\end{cases}}}\right)

(308)

where
\tilde{\nu}=\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y})
.
43
43

43

If we let
\tilde{\mu}=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y})-3\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
and
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})
, the last term in parentheses in
Eq.

308
can be written as
\frac{\tilde{\mu}+\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}}{8}
.

Antisymmetric matter for even
n

As before, an
\mathfrak{su}(n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model with discrete quotient
\nu
can have antisymmetric matter with charge
q=\frac{2\nu}{n}+j
for integers
j
. For even
n
(i.e.,
n=2k
), this means that antisymmetric matter with charge
q
can occur when
\nu
is either
\overline{(kq)}_{n}
or
\overline{(kq+k)}_{n}
. At the codimension-two locus supporting the antisymmetric matter, the
\hat{z}
component vanishes to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{n}{4}q^{2}+\frac{\nu(n-\nu)}{n}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\mu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)\,,

(309)

where

\mu=\overline{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-\frac{2}{n}\nu}}\right)}_{2}+\overline{2\nu}_{4}

(310)

The other section components vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\mu=0\\
(1,2,2)&\mu=2\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1,\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}}}\right)&\mu=1,3\end{cases}\,.

(311)

If one knows the orders of vanishing at the codimension-one
\mathfrak{su}(n)
locus and at the codimension-two antisymmetric locus, the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge of the supported matter can still be determined (up to sign) using
Eq.

308
.

A.3
\mathfrak{so}(2n)

For models with an
\mathfrak{so}(2n)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra, we label the elements of the center of
\Spin(2n)
with the parameter
\nu
, which can run from 0 to
4
. The section components in a model with the quotient corresponding to
\nu
vanish to the following orders at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(2n)

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\nu=0\\
(1,2,2)&\nu=2\\
(1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rfloor,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rceil)&\nu=1,3\end{cases}\,.

(312)

The allowed
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charges for the various
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
representations considered here are summarized in
Table

6
.

Vector matter

At a codimension-two locus supporting
\bm{2n}_{q}
matter, the
\hat{z}
component should vanish to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+\begin{cases}0&\nu=0\\
1&\nu=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\nu=1,3\end{cases}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\\
\frac{n+1}{4}&\mu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)\,,

(313)

where

\mu=\overline{(2q-\nu)}_{4}+\overline{2q}_{2}\,.

(314)

The other section components vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\mu=0\\
(1,2,2)&\mu=2\\
(1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}}}\right\rfloor,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n+1}{2}}}\right\rceil)&\mu=1,3\end{cases}\,.

(315)

Based on these formulas, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
algebra and the codimension-two
\bm{2n}
locus, one can determine the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q
of the supported matter (up to sign) with the formula

q^{2}=2\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})-\begin{cases}0&\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})=0\\
1&\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})=2\\
\frac{n}{4}&\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n}{2}}}\right\rceil\end{cases}+\begin{cases}0&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=0\\
1&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=2\\
\frac{n+1}{4}&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{n+1}{2}}}\right\rceil\end{cases}\,.

(316)

Spinor matter for
\mathfrak{so}(8)

At a codimension-two locus supporting
{\bm{8_{\text{s}}}}_{,q}
or
{\bm{8_{\text{c}}}}_{,q}

\hat{z}

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{2}+\begin{cases}0&\nu=0\\
1&\nu=1,2,3\end{cases}-\begin{cases}0&\mu=0\\
1&\mu=2\\
\frac{5}{4}&\mu=1,3\end{cases}}}\right)\,,

(317)

(318)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\mu=0\\
(1,2,2)&\mu=2\\
(1,2,3)&\mu=1,3\end{cases}\,.

(319)

Based on these formulas, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{so}(8)

\bm{8_{\text{s}}}
or
\bm{8_{\text{c}}}

1&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=2\\
\frac{5}{4}&\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=3\end{cases}

(320)

Spinor matter for
\mathfrak{so}(10)

At a codimension-two locus supporting
\bm{16}_{q}

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{3}q^{2}+\begin{cases}0&\nu=0\\

(324)

Spinor matter for
\mathfrak{so}(12)

\mathfrak{e}_{6}

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,2,3}}\right)\times u_{3}(\nu)\,.

\bm{27}
matter

\mathfrak{e}_{6}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

\bm{27}

for integer

\bm{27}_{q}

\overline{3q}_{3}

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{3}{2}q^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\nu(3-\nu)-\frac{3}{2}\overline{3q}_{2}}}\right)\,,

(332)

The other section components vanish to orders

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,3}}\right)\times\overline{3q}_{2}\,.

Based on these formulas, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{e}_{6}

\bm{27}
locus, one can determine the
\mathfrak{u}(1)

q^{2}={\frac{4}{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})-\frac{\tilde{\nu}(3-\tilde{\nu})}{3}+\frac{\tilde{\mu}}{4}}}\right)}\,,

(334)

where

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\tilde{\nu}=\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x})=\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y})\,,\\
\tilde{\mu}=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y})-3\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})-4\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})\,.\end{gathered}

A.5
\mathfrak{e}_{7}

For models with an
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
gauge algebra, we label the elements of the center of
\gE_{7}
with the parameter
\nu
, which can be either
0
or
1
. The section components in a model with the quotient corresponding to
\nu
vanish to the following orders at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{e}_{7}
algebra:

\ordalt_{1}(\hat{z})=0\,,\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{1}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,3}}\right)\times\nu\,.

(337)

\bm{56}
matter

An
\mathfrak{e}_{7}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
model can have
\bm{56}
matter with
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q=\frac{\nu}{2}+k
for integer
k
. In other words,
\bm{56}_{q}
matter can occur when
\nu
is
\overline{2q}_{2}
. At the codimension-two locus supporting this
\bm{56}_{q}
matter, the
\hat{z}
component should vanish to order

\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})=q^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\nu=q^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\times\overline{2q}_{2}\,,

(338)

while the other section components vanish to orders

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\ordalt_{2}(\hat{x}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{y}),\ordalt_{2}(\hat{w})}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,3,4}}\right)\times\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})\,.

(339)

Based on these formulas, if one knows the orders of vanishing of the section components at the codimension-one locus supporting the
\mathfrak{e}_{7}
algebra and the codimension-two
\bm{56}
locus, one can determine the
\mathfrak{u}(1)
charge
q
of the supported matter (up to sign) as

q^{2}={\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})-\frac{1}{4}\ordalt_{1}(\hat{y})}={\ordalt_{2}(\hat{z})-\frac{1}{4}\ordalt_{1}(\hat{w})}\,.

(340)

Appendix B
The
S
correction term

The results in
StangeEllTrouble
often use the sequence
S_{n}
of numbers in
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
, which is defined as

S_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p,b,d,h,s,w}}\right)=\begin{cases}b^{j}s+\frac{b^{j}-1}{b-1}h+d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{p}(n)-j}}\right)+w&v_{p}(n)>j\\
b^{v_{p}(n)}s+\frac{b^{v_{p}(n)}-1}{b-1}h&v_{p}(n)\leq j\end{cases}\,.

(341)

Here,
v_{p}
is a
p
-adic valuation on
\mathbb{Q}
associated with
p
, while
b
,
d
,
h
,
s
, and
w
are non-negative integers in particular sets:

\displaystyle b\in

\displaystyle p\mathbb{Z}^{>0}\cup\{1\}

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Z}^{>0}

\displaystyle h\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}

\displaystyle s\in

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{>0}\cup\{\infty\}

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}\,.

(342)

The integer
j
is
0
if
b=1
; if
b
is a multiple of
p
, then
j
is the smallest non-negative integer such that

d\leq b^{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(b-1}}\right)s+h}}\right)\,.

(343)

For the examples encountered here,
d
is
v_{p}(p)
, which is
1
. This implies that
j
is
0
regardless of the value of
b
, and the definition of
S_{n}
simplifies to

S_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p,b,v_{p}(p),h,s,w}}\right)=s+v_{p}(n)+\begin{cases}w&v_{p}(n)>0\\
0&v_{p}(n)\leq 0\end{cases}\,.

(344)

The variable
w
is allowed to be 0; in fact, the ideas discussed in Section 5 of
StangeEllTrouble
suggest that, for all of the cases considered here,
w
is expected to be 0. When this is the case, we are left with

S_{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p,b,v_{p}(p),h,s,0}}\right)=s+v_{p}(n)

(345)

If we are interested in using these formulas to explain the order of vanishing patterns, the
v_{p}(n)
term may seem somewhat strange. While the valuation of an integer is a meaningful quantity, one might expect that the order of vanishing of an integer at some locus should be thought of as 0. It therefore seems that, for the purposes of understanding the order-of-vanishing patterns, the
v_{p}(n)
in
S_{n}
should be dropped.

To see if this is a reasonable prescription, it is worth understanding why this
v_{p}(n)
occurs. Recall that
S_{n}
appears in formulas describing the valuations of elliptic divisibility sequences. Therefore, consider an example EDS with the initial values

W_{1}=1\,,\quad W_{2}=2p^{3}\,,\quad W_{3}=8p^{8}-4p^{4}-1\,,\quad W_{4}=4p^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8p^{12}-6p^{4}-1}}\right)\,,

(346)

where
p
is some prime integer. The
W_{n}
with even
n
are proportional to
p
, implying that
n_{P}=2
for this situation. One can then calculate that

W_{6}=-2p^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8p^{8}-4p^{4}-1}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(512p^{24}-768p^{20}+96p^{12}+120p^{8}+36p^{4}+3}}\right)\,.

(347)

For most choices of
p
,
W_{6}
is proportional to
p^{3}
, and
v_{p}(W_{6})
is 3. If, however, we choose
p
to be 3, the last factor in the above expression is a factor of 3, and
v_{p}(W_{6})
enhances to
4
. Similar sorts of
v_{p}(W_{n})
enhancements may occur in other elliptic divisibility sequences, and the
v_{p}(n)
term accounts for this fact. Based on
Eq.

341
, this effect only occurs for particular choices of
n
and
p
. However, this term is not particularly relevant for the analogous situations in elliptic fibrations where valuations are replaced with orders of vanishing; an integer
n
would not vanish at a particular locus in the base. Additionally, note that the examples considered here are formed by starting with an elliptic fibration, setting the parameters involved at a particular locus to some arbitrary prime
p
, setting the other parameters to some integers, and analyzing an EDS corresponding to the resulting elliptic curve. We are interested in the behavior for arbitrary
p
, but for a given value of
n
, the “accidental” increases in
v_{p}(W_{n})
only occur for particular choices of
p
. This effect should therefore be ignored.

Thus, while the
v_{p}(n)
is important for characterizing
p
-adic valuations of elliptic divisibility sequences, it seems that it can be dropped for the purposes of establishing analogies to the orders of vanishing. In other words, if we are looking to justify the expressions we obtain for the order of vanishing, one should be able to replace
S_{n}
with
s
, a positive constant. However, it would be ideal to have a more formal understanding of how to adapt the formulas in
StangeEllTrouble
, particularly the
S
correction term, to scenarios involving the orders of vanishing of section components in elliptic fibrations.

Appendix C
Additional calculations of EDS valuations

In this appendix, we calculate various EDS valuations corresponding to various singularity types using the formulas in
StangeEllTrouble
.

C.1
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-4}
singularities

C.1.1
\nu=1,3
or
\mu=1,3

For models with an
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-4}
locus where
\nu
or
\mu
for the generating section equals 1 or 3, we are interested in the EDS valuations when the components
(\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{z},\hat{w})
vanish to order
(1,k,0,k)
. As an example, we can consider the Weierstrass model in
Eq.

132
, the
\mathfrak{su}(2k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
seed model with
\nu=k+1
used to find order of vanishing data for the antisymmetric representation. The
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-4}
singularities are supported at
\{\sigma=b_{1}=0\}
, so we construct an elliptic curve
E
by setting
\sigma
and
b_{1}
to a prime
p
and setting the remaining parameters to arbitrary numbers. As an example,

can take the form

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}-\frac{p^{2}}{48}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-12+p-4p^{k-2}}}\right)^{2}-48p^{k-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+p}}\right)}}\right]x

(348)

\displaystyle+\frac{p^{3}}{864}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(p-12)^{3}-12(p-12)p^{k-2}(7p-6)+48p^{2k-4}(13p-6)}}\right]\,,

in which case it admits the rational point
P
given by

P\colon(x,y)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{12}(p-12),\frac{5}{6}p^{k}}}\right)\,.

(349)

Since this elliptic curve has additive reduction modulo
p
, we extend the field by the element
p^{\prime}=(p^{1/2})
, let
x\rightarrow{p^{\prime}}^{2}x^{\prime},y\rightarrow{p^{\prime}}^{3}y^{\prime}
, and divide through by
{p^{\prime}}^{6}
. This gives us an elliptic curve
E^{\prime}
of the form

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}^{2}

\displaystyle={x^{\prime}}^{3}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{48}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({p^{\prime}}^{2}-12-4{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right)^{2}+{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+{p^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)}}\right]{x^{\prime}}

(350)

\displaystyle+\Big[\frac{1}{864}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({p^{\prime}}^{2}-12-4{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right)^{3}-\frac{1}{12}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({p^{\prime}}^{2}-12-4{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right){p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+{p^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}{p^{\prime}}^{4k-6}+\frac{2}{27}{p^{\prime}}^{6k-12}\Big]\,,

which admits a rational point

P^{\prime}\colon(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{{p^{\prime}}^{2}}{12}-1,\frac{5}{6}{p^{\prime}}^{2k-3}}}\right)\,.

(351)

with
3{x^{\prime}}^{2}+f^{\prime}
proportional to
{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}
.
The new elliptic curve

has multiplicative reduction modulo
p^{\prime}
with

. Meanwhile, the point

has singular reduction, implying that the EDS valuations are given by

158
with

.
44
44

44

The quantity

is proportional to
{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}

y^{\prime}
is proportional to
{p^{\prime}}^{2k-3}
. The integer

should equal the smaller of these two exponents, which is
2k-4
.
Even multiples of
P^{\prime}
reduce to the identity point with the
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
coordinates of

being proportional to
({p^{\prime}}^{-2},{p^{\prime}}^{-3})

equals

Appendix

B

\displaystyle v(W_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}-1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{m}(2k-4,4k-8)+\begin{cases}1&2\mid m\\
0&2\nmid m\end{cases}}}\right)

(352)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{8}m^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{cases}0&2\mid m\\
\frac{n}{4}&2\nmid m\end{cases}\,.

C.1.2
\nu=2
or
\mu=2

For models with an
\text{I}^{*}_{2k-4}
locus where the generating section has
\nu=2

\mathfrak{su}(2k)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

, which is used to find order of vanishing data for the fundamental and antisymmetric representations of

114

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=x^{3}+p^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-3(1+3p)^{2}+32p^{k-1}+p^{2k-4}(p-1)}}\right)x

(353)

\displaystyle+p^{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2(1+3p)^{3}-32(1+3p)p^{k-1}-(p-1)(7p-2)p^{2k-4}+(19-3p)p^{2k-3}}}\right)\,,

which admits a rational point

P\colon(x,y)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2p(5p-1),4p^{2}(3-7p-p^{k-2})}}\right)\,.

(354)

This elliptic curve has additive reduction modulo
p
. We therefore extend the field by an element
p^{\prime}=(p)^{1/2}
, let

, and divide through by
{p^{\prime}}^{6}
. The resulting elliptic curve

is given by

\displaystyle{y^{\prime}}^{2}

\displaystyle={x^{\prime}}^{3}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-3\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+3{p^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)^{2}-{p^{\prime}}^{4k-8}+{p^{\prime}}^{4k-6}+32{p^{\prime}}^{2k-2}}}\right]{x^{\prime}}

(355)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+3{p^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)^{3}-16{p^{\prime}}^{2k-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+3{p^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right)+{p^{\prime}}^{4k-8}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-1+14{p^{\prime}}^{2}-5{p^{\prime}}^{4}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right]\,,

and it admits a point
P^{\prime}
given by

P^{\prime}\colon(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(5{p^{\prime}}^{2}-1}}\right),4p^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3-7{p^{\prime}}^{2}-{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right)}}\right)\,.

(356)

We can now calculate the EDS valuations according to the formulas in
StangeEllTrouble
. The

155
and
156
. Even multiples of

reduce to the identity point, and the

are respectively proportional to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime}(7{p^{\prime}}^{2}-2)}}\right)^{-2}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime}(7{p^{\prime}}^{2}-2)}}\right)^{-3}

and the

, should be replaced with 1.

The

does not reduce to the singular point, and the EDS valuation calculation therefore involves

157
and
156
. Even multiples of

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-3+7{p^{\prime}}^{2}+{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-2}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\prime}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-3+7{p^{\prime}}^{2}+{p^{\prime}}^{2k-4}}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-3}

, and the

term should be replaced with 1.

Putting everything together, we find that the EDS valuations for
